#3011. AI crush is powering one stock to sky-high valuations

The 2026 midterm elections have profoundly altered the national regulatory environment, most notably with Democrats securing control of both chambers of the U.S. Congress and notching big victories in traditionally business-friendly states like Texas and South Carolina.

Over the past two decades, Southern states like Florida and Texas have attracted massive corporate migration from blue states. With less regulation and lower taxes. Those same benefits also attracted many people seeking lower taxes, lower housing costs, and better economic opportunities. With this political shift, relocated companies may now face a landscape increasingly focused on energy and building constraints, and tighter legislative oversight.

Information technology, semiconductors, and traditional energy sectors experienced immediate headwinds following the election. Companies at the forefront of the AI boom, such as CoreWeave, Tesla, and SpaceX, saw share prices slide as markets priced in tighter Democratic regulatory oversight and potential restrictions on AI deployment. Senator Bernie Sanders, for example, reposted his efforts to advance a data center moratorium proposal he co-authored with New York Congresswoman and socialist Alexandria Ocasio-Cortez.

The market is suddenly realizing that, between shifts in Washington and in states like Texas, the fastest-growing area of the U.S. economy might grind to a halt. Over half of the U.S. hyperscale data center pipeline could now face heightened regulatory friction. To build new facilities, even assuming they get permitted at all, developers may face tax increases, strict "bring your own power" mandates, or new air-permit delays for on-site gas plants. As all-in construction costs rise and timelines extend, immediate demand for advanced chips snowballs down the hardware supply chain.

Traditional oil and gas majors sank, while solar and green energy stocks surged, driven by expectations that a Democratic Congress will resume pushing subsidies for them. Also, the ongoing U.S.-Israel war with Iran has driven crude prices sharply higher throughout the year. With Democrats taking control of Congress, President Trump faces a legislative body that can severely constrain his military options and push back on defense budgets. Tehran almost certainly recognized that a politically weakened Trump administration would have a greater incentive to seek a negotiated deal, something Secretary of State Marco Rubio has now indicated is preferable to any escalating kinetic options. Consequently, crude prices plunged, with Brent dropping below $95 per barrel.

For similar reasons, defense contractors took market hits due to anticipated congressional resistance to the $1.5 trillion Pentagon budget. Although healthcare - as measured by XLV at least came in today, it remains above pre-election levels, along with grocery stocks such as Walmart, Costco, Kroger, and Dollar General, on expectations that a Democratic Congress might boost federal spending in those areas.

This may help explain why Costco was one of the relatively few stocks to close higher on Thursday, and why it also ranked as the 15th most active single-stock option (in notional terms), ahead of names like JP Morgan, Marvell, Oracle, Eli Lilly, and Dell.

At roughly 46x forward earnings, Costco (a perennial on the "Holly Index (MELLOY, CAN WE LINK TO THIS") isn't cheap, but then it is never cheap. The average forward multiple is actually a few turns higher at over 49x. So what were folks trading? Mostly short-dated call options. One possible reason? Costco isn't just trading at a relatively high multiple; as usual, it is also simply a high-priced stock in dollar terms, at over $1,065 as of Thursday's close. A "round lot" (100 shares) would therefore cost over $100,000 to initiate. Like many things packaged at Costco, that might be a little more than some buyers need (or want) to spend.

Alternatively, call options let investors gain exposure to 100 shares for far less than buying the shares. Buying the January 1060 calls, for example, would cost about $53.45, or $5,345 for upside participation on 100 shares of stock above the $1,060 strike price. That may seem more palatable, but a keen observer might notice that prior highs occasionally present some technical resistance, and Costco topped out at about $1,090 back in mid-May. By selling a call at or near that level, such as the January 1090s at 39.65, one could reduce the cost of the trade even further, expecting a pause before new highs. This would reduce the cost of the trade to $13.80, or just under 1.3% of the current stock price.

I won't pretend $1,380 is a small outlay for a one-lot call spread on a much-loved stock trading at 46x forward earnings. Nor is $520 for 8 6-ounce A5 Wagyu filet mignons (a Costco online only deal available now). But relatively, both the steaks at Costco and the options on Costco are very fairly priced. But it does provide participation in 100 shares of Costco between the strikes, for only 30% more than the cost of a single share of the underlying stock.

#3002. Top House Oversight Democrat warns Trump family to 'lawyer up'

Rep. Robert Garcia, the California Democrat poised to lead the powerful House Oversight Committee, doubled down on his warning to President Donald Trump and his allies Friday, releasing a video promising sweeping investigations into the administration.

"Oversight Democrats are ready to take on the crooks, the con men and the Trump crime family. Lawyer up," the California Democrat wrote on X.

"The American people have spoken, and they are tired of the unprecedented level of corruption coming out of this White House and under President Trump," Garcia said in the video, which ends with the words "LAWYER UP" splashed across the screen.

The warning comes three days after Democrats' sweeping midterm victories handed them control of the House. When the new Congress convenes in January, Democrats will gain control of the committee's subpoena power, allowing them to compel testimony and obtain documents in investigations that could target Trump, his family and his allies.

Garcia said the committee would investigate what he described as potential corruption involving the Trump family's business dealings, cryptocurrency ventures and Pentagon contracts, as well as alleged abuses by Immigration and Customs Enforcement. He also vowed to scrutinize individuals he said were connected to convicted sex offender Jeffrey Epstein.

"Oversight Democrats are absolutely going to ensure that we investigate every single one of these instances," Garcia said. "Now let's get to work."

Garcia's approach may conflict with what some advisors are telling Democrats — to go after corporate interests rather than Trump and his family.

Garcia, currently the committee's top Democrat, formally launched his bid to become chairman Thursday. He is widely expected to secure the position.

The White House has dismissed Democrats' planned investigations as politically motivated, setting up a potentially contentious showdown between the Trump administration and the incoming Democratic congressional majority.

The White House did not immediately respond to CNBC's request for comment.

#2999. Data center double-down: Hassett says blue wave won't stop AI build-out

National Economic Council Director Kevin Hassett said Friday he expects data center growth to continue as projected, despite the blue-wave election that saw voters overwhelmingly favor limiting the tech infrastructure buildout.

Hassett, on CNBC's "Squawk on the Street," acknowledged the dozens of measures on states' midterm ballots seeking to constrain data centers, a crucial part of President Donald Trump's push to win the global artificial intelligence race.

In at least 46 of the roughly 50 such initiatives on ballots in 2026, majorities voted to restrict development of the facilities, according to Ballotpedia's tally.

"We respect the localities that decide that's what they want to do," Hassett said. "But America is a big place, and right now, the data centers scheduled over the next couple of years aren't affected by these ballot initiatives."

"And the stuff after that, you know, there's plenty of room in the U.S. for data center growth to continue, as it was projected ahead of the election," he said.

Hassett said he expects data centers will continue to expand in states with more welcoming governors, and he suggested that even hostile states will eventually come around.

"That's exactly what's going to happen," he said. "That, or what'll happen will be that the benefits of the data centers will be so obvious that people decide to change their minds about the way they regulate them."

Hassett also blamed Thursday's tech-driven market plunge on "uncertainty about what the political change is going to mean for data centers going forward."

"We've now got a ... new majority that maybe is going to go in there and muddle around in there, and I think that markets are anxious about that," he said.

"But I think that the economic strength that we see everywhere else is so strong, and of course, in the end, you could take something that's growing as fast as the data centers and make it grow a little bit slower, and you still have a blockbuster economy."

"And so, I think we're still very, very optimistic about what's going to happen going forward," he said.

The CNBC Voter Poll found 56% of midterm voters identified data centers as either a crucial or major issue — and of those who considered it crucial, 62% favored the generic Democratic candidate.

Trump has strongly embraced AI and data centers and has even tried to reverse their seeming unpopularity by rebranding the tech as "super intelligence," or SI.

In a Truth Social post Friday, Trump made clear that despite the midterm losses, his support for the nascent industry has not faltered.

"As President, I am 100% in favor of Super Intelligence (SI), and will work with the many States in the U.S. that want to become RICH by having them," Trump wrote. "They are GOLD, and only foolish leadership will say 'NO' to the riches they bring!"

#2997. LeBron's return to Cleveland highlights a new reality: Sixers' offense is a work in progress

CLEVELAND — About an hour before tipoff, 76ers forward LeBron James emerged from the visitors' tunnel, with a collection of fans gathered near the southeast corner of Rocket Arena cheering triumphantly. It was impossible to ignore what could have been.

It was like a scene from the Apple TV series "Dark Matter," where the protagonist finds himself in an alternate reality and ponders the possibility of playing it out. A world exists, somewhere in the basketball multiverse, where James decides to ride off into the sunset donning the Cavaliers' wine and gold — not the 76ers' red, white and blue.

On Friday night, aside from the very obvious fact that James indeed plays for Philadelphia now, everything else felt normal in Cleveland: the pregame warm-up, rife with greetings and on-court drills, the same ones he had done in this very building for years; the chalk toss in front of the Cavaliers' faithful. Even the in-arena announcer's energy when James was introduced last was met with raucous applause. And when he checked out with four minutes remaining, a standing ovation ensued.

But the most prominent theme from "Dark Matter" is that no matter how familiar a place looks and feels, it's not your reality. And James was reminded of that.

"Every game has its own challenges," James said following the Sixers' 119-95 loss. "I don't think [we were sharp] offensively as a group and I know I damn sure wasn't good at all. Look at the film and try to get better for Sunday."

It would be somewhat nit-picky to overly dissect the 76ers' performance Friday after an 8-2 start — especially considering how much doubt and uncertainty existed about multiple superstars co-existing. But something has to be said about an offense (which ranked 21st in efficiency before tip-off and will continue to drop after mustering only 95 points) that has yet to jell.

In its second meeting with Cleveland, Philly didn't operate overwhelmingly loose with the ball (with 16 total turnovers), but there was no purpose to its ball movement. Most of the Sixers' half-court actions were stagnant and head coach Nick Nurse's rotations — designed to stagger their superpowers — only led to less flow and control.

Joel Embiid, who finished with just six points on 0-for-9 shooting, continued his floor-spacing woes. He's now shooting an abysmal 3-for-28 from deep, which puts even more pressure on the likes of James, Tyrese Maxey and Jaylen Brown to keep defenses honest. The 76ers won't win many games where Embiid is that inefficient and dominates the ball like he did, with a whopping 41.7 usage rate in the opening 12 minutes.

"I believe in myself," Embiid said. "But when you're in these moments and nothing is going in, it's hard to not look at yourself and be down. But you have to keep going and working."

But isn't this why LeBron came here in the first place? It wasn't because the 76ers were necessarily seeking another offensive weapon — they were in desperate need of his offensive mind.

Coming into Friday night's game, James ranked in the 96th percentile in assist rate and his assist-to-usage ratio was at the highest of his career. Conventional logic says maybe give the guy who's among the league leaders in potential assists, rim assist rate and offensive RAPM the ball as much as possible (his current output is 10% lower than his career usage) — as well as keep Maxey on the floor as much as possible (Philly's already woeful offense falls off a cliff when he sits).

Some of their playmaking metrics are noisy, partially because the sample size is still small after only 10 games and partially because of their sheer star power. For instance, of lineups that have played at least 50 minutes together, the 76ers' starters are taking good shots (seventh in shot quality per PBB Stats). They're also eighth in passes made per game and seventh in secondary assists, which indicates a concerted effort to move the ball and generate good looks. This, to me, is more important than ranking 18th in assists and 21st in potential assists, both of which stem from shot-making luck.

Friday was an outlier in that James coughed the ball up six times, but his other offensive struggles — eight points on 12 shots — weren't that much different than what the 41-year-old has endured this season, with the lowest points per shot attempt of a long, successful career.

"Too many turnovers on my behalf," James said. "And I didn't put in a rhythm offensively all game. Couple of them were careless, couple of them were trying to make the right play and not being successful with it."

The 76ers have started the season on a high note and, thanks to an impressive, aggressive defense, have silenced a few naysayers in the opening weeks of the new campaign. But until their offense can find a happy medium, one that keeps James at the mental wheel and highlights the other stars in their respective roles — or if he can rediscover his scoring efficiency — their dreams of serious contention could be another alternate reality door.

#2995. Microsoft plans to use fewer neoclouds as AI buildout shows signs of strain

Microsoft is shrinking its roster of neocloud partners as it focuses on work with a few key suppliers and builds out its own data centers, according to people familiar with the matter.

The software giant's plans pose another potential challenge for a corner of the artificial intelligence market that's showing signs of strain due to rising borrowing costs, hefty debt loads and concerns about whether the buildout will slow down.

Microsoft has forged relationships in recent years with a number of neoclouds, including CoreWeave, Nebius, Chronoscale, IREN, Lambda and Nscale, as well as with Oracle, which is pouring money into building out AI infrastructure.

Microsoft is now in the evaluation stage and hasn't decided which neoclouds it will stop using, said two sources, who asked not to be named because the talks are confidential. One of the people said Microsoft wants to prioritize its own data center footprint. Bloomberg reported on the plans earlier. Two people said that Microsoft opted not to renew a deal with Oracle.

A Microsoft spokesperson said in an emailed statement that its infrastructure strategy is "built on flexibility and choice."

"We combine Microsoft-owned datacenters, leased facilities and strategic partnerships with third-party providers to deliver the capacity our customers need, where and when they need it," the spokesperson said. "We regularly review these investments against customer demand. That includes tracking delivery and adjusting plans as circumstances change."

The neoclouds have been a turbulent trade on Wall Street in recent weeks as rising interest rates added to fears that many companies have become overly reliant on debt in rushing to build capacity. Shares of CoreWeave, Nebius and IREN are all down by roughly half over the past month even after rallying on Friday.

A recent industry review counted 323 neoclouds, and experts have predicted that many of them won't survive.

In its earnings report on Thursday, IREN said it handed over data center capacity to Microsoft in August, but Microsoft requested a networking equipment upgrade that will result in the infrastructure coming online in January or February instead of this year.

Microsoft finance chief Amy Hood said on the software company's fiscal first-quarter earnings call in October that some cloud contracts involving graphics processing units were delayed until the first half of 2027, affecting Azure cloud growth. Microsoft reported $45.2 billion worth of capital expenditures and finance leases during the latest quarter, and said capex spending will accelerate in the second half of the fiscal year.

CoreWeave and Nebius are both scheduled to report quarterly results next week. IREN shares popped 16% on Friday after the company said AI revenue more than doubled from the prior quarter to $200 million. IREN co-CEO Daniel Roberts told CNBC that there's a "disconnect between what we're seeing in public market and what we're seeing at the customer level."

However, he acknowledged the challenge that market conditions present.

"Capital markets have become more selective in the last few weeks, and the questions have undoubtedly moved from how big the contract is to whether it can be delivered, financed, and run at a profit," Roberts said on the earnings call.

Microsoft has already slashed its work with U.K.-based neocloud Nscale, walking away from a site that it had intended to use in England. Darren Hardman, Microsoft's CEO for the U.K and Ireland, told CNBC in October that the decision had to do with Nscale's ability to get access to electricity.

The Financial Times reported this week that Microsoft decided against renewing a contract with Nscale in Texas. Nscale filed to go public in mid-September but hasn't updated its prospectus since then. The company said in its IPO filing that it expects Microsoft to be a "significant customer in future periods."

Microsoft's big turn to neoclouds came after the launch of ChatGPT in 2022, which led to a surge in demand for GPUs. In looking elsewhere, Microsoft signed up CoreWeave, which was a privately held startup at the time with roots in cryptocurrency mining. When CoreWeave went public last year, it said 62% of its 2024 revenue came from Microsoft.

At the time of ChatGPT's release, OpenAI only used Azure. Now the company's cloud suppliers also include Amazon, Cerebras, CoreWeave, Google and Oracle.

Microsoft's spokesperson said that its approach to reviewing neocloud investments is "a normal and responsible way to manage large, complex infrastructure projects," and that "the pace of our new agreements reflects what we need today."

—CNBC's Kif Leswing contributed to this report.

#2993. Warriors star reveals eye-opening ‘short-man complex’ admission, fueling rage on court

Just as Draymond Green was coming around on Dillon Brooks, the yearslong feud between two of the NBA's most controversial players was reignited this week, when Brooks came to defend his teammate and Green, seething, grabbed him by the collar of his jersey.

Green was ejected from the Warriors' 116-113 win over the Suns on Monday night. Brooks said immediately following the game that he was trying to "de-escalate" the situation, which began when Green wrapped both arms around Khaman Maluach and threw him to the floor.

Addressing the situation for the first time with reporters after Golden State's 120-100 win over the Heat on Thursday night, Green called out Brooks' point of view on their latest altercation.

"He was talking. He came in talking. Putting his chest on my shoulder. That ain't how you de-escalate," Green said. "So I just wish he stood on the type of business he was on, instead of running to y'all. Like, 'I was trying to de-escalate!' No you wasn't, bro."

Golden State Warriors' Draymond Green (23) and Phoenix Suns' Jalen Bridges (15) get into an altercation on Monday, Nov. 2, 2026, in San Francisco. JOSE CARLOS FAJARDO/Bay Area News Group via AP

Golden State Warriors' Draymond Green, left, throws down Phoenix Suns' Khaman Maluach in San Francisco, Monday, Nov. 2, 2026. JOSE CARLOS FAJARDO/Bay Area News Group via AP

Both players like to play the villain, with their rivalry going back to Brooks' time with Memphis. A year after their antics gained national attention during the heated 2022 Western Conference semifinals, Brooks took it to the next level, telling ESPN, "I don't like Draymond at all."

Green this week, however, said that he "had started to turn a corner" on his view of Brooks.

"Ah, man, he may be who he say he is," Green said. "But he not."

Brooks' actions and response to the altercation only confirmed Green's initial belief — that Brooks' tough-guy persona only exists so long as the cameras and microphones are on.

"The words you said on the side of me and walking up to me like that," Green said. "When you're de-escalating you usually go to your teammate. But your teammate's there [on the ground]. So you're not de-escalating anything.

"By the way, there's nothing going on. He's there, I'm standing there. There's nothing really to de-escalate."

It depends on your point of view. The scene underneath the basket was reasonably docile until Brooks ran toward Green and shoved him in the back. Green responded by turning around and grabbing Brooks by the jersey near the neck. But Brooks' teammate had already been thrown down by Green.

The sequence earned Green two technical fouls, resulting in his ejection. The Warriors, who were trailing by 11 at the time, said it helped fire them up on their way to a comeback win.

But Green gave an even more telling look into his worldview with his explanation.

"You've heard of people having short man complex? What's it called–Napoleon? I got a little bit of that when I'm playing basketball," said Green, listed at 6-foot-6 and 235 pounds, "because I'm always smaller than the guys I'm going up against."

Golden State Warriors forward Draymond Green argues a call during the first half of an NBA basketball game against the Miami Heat, Thursday, Nov. 5, 2026. AP Photo/Tony Avelar

Despite his size disadvantage, at age 36, Green has more or less played shutdown defense while primarily playing the five this season, a result of Yaxel Lendeborg's emergence at the four.

Matched up against Green Thursday night, Giannis Antetokounmpo (6-11, 250) struggled to steamroll into the paint and finished 4-of-9 from the field. When matched up against anyone else, Antetokounmpo converted 3 of 4 attempts from the field and got to the foul line twice.

Green has similarly more than held his own against Rudy Gobert (7-1, 258), Walker Kessler (7-2, 245) and Nikola Jokić (6-11, 284). He's got to find an edge somehow.

"I'm usually outweighed by anywhere from 20 to 60 pounds. I'm usually outsized from a height perspective, anywhere from 5 to Wemby," Green said, eliciting laughs. "With that, In order to do what I do, you have to have that certain nastiness to you. …

Draymond Green of the Golden State Warriors helps up Stephen Curry during a game against the Miami Heat at Chase Center in San Francisco on Thursday, Nov. 5, 2026. NBAE via Getty Images

"I have to have that tenacity. There's certain things you cannot allow to happen because if you allow it to happen the next game you play, it's gonna happen again. Somebody's gonna try it again."

And, so, that's why Green felt compelled to do more than just prevent Maluach (7-2, 250) from converting the entry pass he received in the paint. He had to send a message.

And, like the Warriors' hopes that night, the rivalry was rekindled, just like that.

#2991. OpenAI, Arm and Qualcomm executives react to post-midterm AI sell-off

As a post-midterm rally Wednesday turned into a steep sell-off Thursday, tech executives sought to look through the notion that potential data center constraints could be catastrophic for their businesses.

In response to a Democratic sweep of Congress as well as a sizeable number of state governorships and legislatures in the midterm election Tuesday night, stocks initially rallied Wednesday, posting their best day in more than 18 months as President Trump accepted the blowout results with little fanfare.

The tide quickly turned Thursday, however, with stocks selling off sharply as investors feared a potential slowdown in the data center build-out after wins by candidates in favor of data center regulation in states like Texas and the possibility that the artificial intelligence trade may be past its peak. Tech led the sell-off, with the "Magnificent Seven" losing over $1 trillion in aggregate market cap.

Stocks recovered some of their losses Friday.

Here's how tech executives reacted to the midterms and sell-off threat:

Haas characterized the postelection fear as "very, very short term thinking" and not likely to be a major threat to Arm's business in the long term, but said the U.S. must keep investing in the data center build-out before the rest of the world catches up.

"If the U.S. hesitates in any way, shape, or form in terms of data center build-out, the rest of the world is ready and willing. There is just huge demand for this capacity," Haas told CNBC's "Squawk on the Street" Thursday.

Friar told CNBC's Sara Eisen Friday that OpenAI will continue investing in data center infrastructure after voters resoundly rejected data centers in their backyards during the midterm election. The company will also focus on community concerns, including electricity. "We will never raise your electricity bill," Friar said."When I think about the midterms, I need to keep thinking about how do we keep investing to meet that customer demand. It starts early with having built relationships with all of the hyperscalers, and it will continue to mean that we raise capital," Friar said.

"If the midterm means more focus on data centers and communities, we're going to keep doing the work to get into those communities and to build trust with them."

Amon said the anxiety about the future of the data center build-out doesn't pose a significant threat to the chipmaker's business, as data center infrastructure is just one segment. Qualcomm slid 9.49% in Thursday's sell-off before recovering some losses Friday.

"The whole market is down, but I think the Qualcomm story is a little bit different," Amon told CNBC's Jon Fortt Thursday. Unlike traditional PC and data center chipmakers, Qualcomm has historically focused on low-power consumption computing. "The story of Qualcomm is what we have done outside that on the data center specific question."

Levie told "Squawk on the Street" Thursday that productivity gains meant AI "is clearly going in one direction," but said a tendency for industry messaging to coalesce around "doomerism" can exacerbate the public perception problems AI companies face.

"I think it's a little bit easier to rotate it toward maybe the more risk-oriented messages, and that's something that our industry needs to work on clearly. But ultimately, obviously, the consumer needs to see the direct benefit, and that's something that will just take time," said Box's Levie.

Risher said the dour market sentiment wasn't concerning for Lyft's business, as consumer sentiment and bookings remained strong. "I wouldn't get too distracted about the short term stuff of what's going on in the market. If I look at the consumer, for example, we just had the biggest Halloween we've ever had," Risher told CNBC's "Squawk Box" Friday.

#2990. Oklahoma State's balanced offense overwhelms Queens in blowout victory

STILLWATER, Okla. — Oklahoma State men's basketball put its offensive depth and unselfish play on full display Friday night, cruising to a 118-68 victory over Queens behind a barrage of three-pointers and contributions throughout the lineup.

The Cowboys (2-0) finished with 30 assists on 44 made field goals, shot 62% from the field and connected on 16 of 28 three-point attempts. Andrija Grbovic led Oklahoma State with 17 points, while Parker Robinson added 14, Julius Halaifonua scored 13 and Latrell Almond finished with 12. Jordan Walker contributed 11 points, and Ryan Crotty added 10.

Queens entered the matchup with a 2-0 record after averaging 95.5 points through its first two games. The Royals also returned four players from last season's Atlantic Sun championship team that reached the NCAA Tournament. However, Oklahoma State quickly established control with its transition offense, perimeter shooting and ball movement.

Halaifonua scored the Cowboys' first two field goals, connecting on a three-pointer before finishing inside. Kashie Natt followed with a putback, and Jordan Burks threw down a transition dunk off a steal to give Oklahoma State a 10-6 advantage.

The Cowboys began separating themselves midway through the first half as Grbovic caught fire from beyond the arc. After connecting on a corner three-pointer, Grbovic added another from the wing before knocking down consecutive triples to extend Oklahoma State's lead to 30-10.

The offensive explosion featured several highlight-reel finishes. Almond threw down a powerful dunk off a flashy pass from Natt, while Anthony Felesi finished another slam following an assist from Kayne Cleary. Walker added a wide-open three-pointer, and Burks later finished a transition dunk off a full-court pass from Luka Bogavac. Oklahoma State carried a 52-31 advantage into halftime, shooting 48.7% from the field and 53.8% from three-point range.

Perhaps the most impressive first-half numbers were the Cowboys' 15 assists on 19 made baskets and 25 points from their bench. Queens, meanwhile, struggled to find its shooting touch, connecting on just 2 of 17 three-point attempts before halftime.

Any hopes of a second-half comeback quickly disappeared. Natt scored on a drive to the basket just 16 seconds into the half. Felesi then connected with Walker for an alley-oop dunk in transition, and Bogavac added a fast-break layup. Almond followed with two dunks, including one off a steal, as Oklahoma State opened the half with 14 unanswered points to extend its advantage to 66-31.

The Cowboys' lead reached 40 points with approximately 14 minutes remaining, allowing coach Steve Lutz to continue utilizing his bench. Oklahoma State surpassed the century mark when Halaifonua connected on a three-pointer with 7:44 remaining, pushing the lead to 101-53. The Cowboys continued scoring throughout the closing minutes before completing the 50-point victory.

Nearly every Oklahoma State player who entered the game scored. Cleary was the lone exception, playing six minutes as he works his way back from an offseason injury under a minutes restriction.

Oklahoma State's final shooting numbers reflected its offensive dominance. The Cowboys shot 62% overall, 57.1% from three-point range and 14-of-19 at the free-throw line. The 30 assists were another indication of the team's offensive chemistry, with Oklahoma State consistently finding open shooters and creating opportunities in transition.

After opening the season with a 91-60 victory over Incarnate Word, the Cowboys have now scored 209 points through their first two games.

While tougher competition awaits, including an upcoming road matchup against Texas A&M, Oklahoma State has demonstrated its scoring potential and depth during an impressive opening week. For Lutz and the Cowboys, Friday's performance provided another encouraging glimpse of what their new-look roster can accomplish when the offense is clicking.

#2989. OpenAI will keep investing in AI data centers despite election results, CFO tells CNBC

OpenAI CFO Sarah Friar on Friday said the company will continue to invest in artificial intelligence infrastructure, even after voters strongly opposed data centers during Tuesday's midterm elections.

"When I think about the midterms, I need to keep thinking about how do we keep investing to meet that customer demand," Friar told CNBC's Sara Eisen in an interview at the Citi TMT Leadership Summit in Pebble Beach, California. "It starts early with having built relationships with all of the hyperscalers, and it will continue to mean that we raise capital."

Out of 41 local ballot measures across 10 states, voters backed restrictions or rejected pro-development measures in all but one, according to an analysis by Ballotpedia and additional results gathered by CNBC on Wednesday. Opposition to the facilities mounted swiftly over the summer as Americans grew worried about their potential to harm the environment, raise utility bills and contribute to job loss.

Investors have turned skeptical on the AI trade of late due to a combination of higher borrowing costs, concerns about soaring debt and public backlash toward the industry. The Nasdaq on Thursday suffered its steepest drop in 19 months, led by a plunge in shares of the chipmakers and neocloud companies most closely tied to the AI buildout. The stocks rebounded some on Friday.

OpenAI depends on data centers to train its AI models and serve customers, and Friar said she doesn't think the election results dramatically changed anything for the company. On Thursday, OpenAI published a policy blueprint and a series of commitments that it pledged to apply to company-owned and -operated data centers in the future.

"If the midterm means more focus on data centers and communities, we're going to keep doing the work to get into those communities and to build trust with them," Friar said.

OpenAI confidentially filed its prospectus with the Securities and Exchange Commission in June, and Friar said Friday that the company would continue to look into a 2027 debut. Anthropic, OpenAI's chief rival, is trying to go public first, with an aim to hit the Nasdaq in mid-November.

Anthropic's revenue in the first nine months of the year jumped roughly 1,200% to $33.2 billion, according to its prospectus. The company said it has entered into purchase commitments and lease agreements with an aggregate committed spend of about $895.4 billion, "substantially all of which is related to compute and infrastructure."

OpenAI is in talks to raise funding at a $1.25 billion pre-money valuation, Friar said Friday. She said "a good CFO always raises money when she can" and that the round is ongoing.

The company has closed an initial $11 billion portion of that funding round, CNBC confirmed on Friday, and it could raise up to an additional $9 billion in the coming weeks. Bloomberg was first to report the $11 billion portion.

"The demand for AI is real," Friar said. "I don't think anyone's arguing with that, or if they are, I think it's a losing argument."

#2986. The midterm election could reshape the muni bond market. Here’s what investors need to know

The outcome of Tuesday's election is expected to have broad implications across the market, including municipal bonds.

The spotlight has been on the battle for congressional control and whether Democrats will wrest the House of Representatives, the Senate or both from Republicans. But for the muni market, what happens at the state level could have bigger repercussions — at least in the near term.

There is an array of initiatives on ballots around the nation that could change tax laws and add to bond supply. Taxes matter to muni investors since the income received is free of federal tax, as well as state tax if the holder lives in the state where the bond is issued.

Depending on the location, ballot measures are up for approval that would either provide tax breaks, or raise taxes on the wealthy.

"It's a complete bifurcation between red and blue states," said Dan Close, head of municipals at Nuveen. "Blue states are trying to put in place graduated income tax structures, trying to capture more revenue from high-income earners. Red states are promoting affordability by trying to cut property taxes."

Red states are those that predominantly vote Republican, while blue states swing toward Democrats.

For instance, California has a proposition known as the "billionaire tax," which would impose a one-time levy equal to 5% of the wealth of certain taxpayers with at least $1 billion in assets. The state also has a measure on the ballot that would make a temporary tax hike permanent on individuals earning over $371,000, adjusted annually for inflation. The tax is currently set to expire in 2031.

"The impact to the muni bond market will be to make the exemption more valuable," Close said of the possible passage of tax increases. Investors will likely seek shelter in the muni market, which would send bond prices higher, he noted.

It is a different story in Florida, where voters are set to decide whether to increase the Sunshine State's homestead exemption for non-school district property taxes. Qualified homeowners currently have a $25,000 property tax exemption, as well as another for $25,000 that doesn't apply to school property taxes. The new proposal raises the latter exemption to $150,000 in 2027 and $250,000 by 2028. New residents have a five-year waiting period before receiving the benefit.

If passed, some local governments will have to find other revenue sources or increase the overall tax rate to replace what they've lost, said Tom Kozlik, head of public policy and municipal strategy at HilltopSecurities.

"It's not necessarily going to result in downgrades for sure, but there would have to be some balancing with regard to overall revenues," he said.

Not all municipalities will be hurt equally, which means credit research is important, said Close.

"Like everything in municipal finance, it is really on a case-by-case basis that you could have some credit deterioration from [a] rollback of property taxes," he said.

Other voters on Tuesday will be asked to approve more state or local government debt. That could weigh on a market that has already seen heightened supply.

Some $473.8 billion has already been issued year to date, through September, according to the Securities Industry and Financial Markets Association. That's a 7.8% increase over the same period a year ago. In 2025, total muni issuance reached $587.3 billion, 14.3% higher than 2024, Sifma said.

Cooper Howard, director of fixed income research and strategy at the Schwab Center for Financial Research, believes supply is the biggest issue coming out of the election.

"That amount of issuance has been one of the reasons why you have to see that strong amount of demand continue to keep pace with it for total returns to keep up," Howard said.

Fortunately, new supply doesn't necessarily come quickly into the market. "There's a window over a couple years that they can issue," he noted.

The ultimate makeup of the next Congress could also have consequences for muni investors as lawmakers sort out their priorities. Most notable is what lawmakers will do to tackle the country's affordability problem, Kozlik at Hilltop said.

"There could be some tax policy initiatives and even affordability-related initiatives that manifest themselves in the first quarter of next year," he said. "That could impact housing. It could impact higher education. It could impact public power financing."

Investors considering moves before the election may want to look at those states with tax increases on the ballot, said Close at Nuveen.

For instance, Nuveen recently began offering a separately managed account dedicated to Washington after getting an enormous number of inquiries from financial advisors. The state recently enacted a 9.9% tax on individuals with taxable annual income above $1 million, set to go into effect in 2028. But the issue is now being put to voters, who can decide whether to repeal the new law.

"At the margin, if you do think that some of these measures are going to pass, knowing that bonds usually move up with an increase in taxes, there might be some opportunities to buy ahead of the election for blue states," Close said.

Howard believes munis are attractive right now, particularly for those who are in a tax bracket of 24% or above. Fundamentals are good and the bonds largely have good credit quality, he said. "About 70% of the broad muni index is either triple-A or double-A rated."

Kozlik is also bullish, calling yields "really attractive." He suggests investors concentrate on AA-rated or higher bonds.

"The muni space is part of the portfolio that most investors want," he said. "It helps them sleep well at night."

#2985. Warriors Get Concerning Injury News on 3 Key Players Amid Win Streak

SAN FRANCISCO, CALIFORNIA - OCTOBER 23: Draymond Green #23 of the Golden State Warriors stands on the court during their game against the Memphis Grizzlies at Chase Center on October 23, 2026 in San Francisco, California. NOTE TO USER: User expressly acknowledges and agrees that, by downloading and/or using this photograph, user is consenting to the terms and conditions of the Getty Images License Agreement. (Photo by Ezra Shaw/Getty Images) Getty

LOS ANGELES, CALIFORNIA - OCTOBER 21: Gui Santos #15 of the Golden State Warriors battles Sandro Mamukelashvili #54 of the Los Angeles Lakers for position during the second half of a game at Crypto.com Arena on October 21, 2026 in Los Angeles, California. NOTE TO USER: User expressly acknowledges and agrees that, by downloading and or using this photograph, User is consenting to the terms and conditions of the Getty Images License Agreement. (Photo by Sean M. Haffey/Getty Images) Getty

The Golden State Warriors have spent the past two weeks making their mounting injury problems look manageable. Their latest injury report suggests that could become considerably harder.

Draymond Green, Gary Payton II and Gui Santos are all questionable for Saturday's game against the Orlando Magic at Chase Center, according to the NBA's official injury report released Friday.

Green is dealing with right elbow inflammation and soreness, Payton has right calf tightness, and Santos continues to manage left knee tendinitis.

The uncertainty arrives as Golden State rides a six-game winning streak following a 0-3 start, with coach Steve Kerr potentially facing another round of rotation adjustments.

Getty Draymond Green of the Golden State Warriors stands on the court during their game against the Memphis Grizzlies at Chase Center on October 23, 2026 in San Francisco, California.

Green's status is perhaps the most consequential development.

The four-time NBA champion has anchored Golden State's undersized frontcourt while serving as its defensive organizer and primary facilitator alongside Stephen Curry.

He delivered another example Thursday, recording 11 assists, five rebounds, a block and a steal in the Warriors' 120-100 victory over the Miami Heat.

Green scored only two points, but his impact extended well beyond his shooting numbers.

Kerr has repeatedly emphasized that the veteran's scoring is not a priority, particularly given his responsibilities defensively.

The Warriors have increasingly deployed Green at center, relying on his positioning and communication to compensate for their lack of traditional size.

His potential absence would leave Al Horford and Charles Bassey with greater responsibility inside.

Green has already missed two games this season, and his latest designation raises the possibility of a third.

GettyGui Santos of the Golden State Warriors battles Sandro Mamukelashvili of the Los Angeles Lakers for position during the second half of a game at Crypto.com Arena on October 21, 2026 in Los Angeles, California.

Golden State also faces uncertainty along the perimeter.

Payton's right calf soreness threatens to remove one of Kerr's most dependable point-of-attack defenders.

The veteran guard gives Golden State a disruptive presence against opposing ballhandlers, often creating transition opportunities through his defensive pressure.

Without him, De'Anthony Melton and Brandon Williams could receive additional minutes.

Santos, meanwhile, is attempting to return after missing the Warriors' previous two games.

The Brazilian forward participated in Friday's practice and was feeling better, according to Nick Friedell of The Athletic .

That represents progress after left knee tendinitis forced him to miss Golden State's victories over Phoenix and Miami.

Santos is averaging 7.2 points, four rebounds and 1.3 assists through six appearances.

Will Richard started Thursday in his absence and could remain in the lineup if Santos cannot play.

The three questionable designations add to an injury report that already includes several significant absences.

Jimmy Butler remains sidelined following right ACL surgery, while Moses Moody continues recovering from left patellar tendon surgery.

Kristaps Porzingis is also out after announcing Friday that he would step away from basketball to prioritize his health.

Despite those setbacks, Golden State has climbed to 6-3 behind Curry's scoring and a productive bench.

The Warriors' reserves outscored Miami's 53-27 on Thursday, helping extend a winning streak that has transformed their early-season outlook.

Orlando arrives with injury problems of its own.

Paolo Banchero, Jalen Suggs and Jonathan Isaac are among five Magic players already ruled out for Saturday.

The Warriors will have an opportunity to continue their surge against a depleted opponent.

Whether they can do so with Green, Payton and Santos remains uncertain.

Their final availability will help determine just how much depth Kerr must call upon Saturday night.

#2984. Sen. Chris Murphy released from hospital after stroke, will miss votes for 'several weeks' as he rehabs

Connecticut Democratic Sen. Chris Murphy said Friday he had been released from a hospital after suffering a stroke last weekend.

Murphy, 53, said he will be in a rehabilitation center for the next "several weeks," meaning he will miss votes in the Senate when Congress returns on Monday.

"The doctors are very encouraged by the progress I've made so far," he said in a statement.

"In order to take my recovery seriously and get back to work on the quickest timeline possible, I will be spending several weeks at a rehabilitation center after being discharged from the hospital today," said Murphy, who has been discussed as a possible presidential candidate in 2028.

Dr. Jose Suarez, chief of Neurosciences Critical Care at The Johns Hopkins Hospital, in a statement released by Murphy's office, said, "Senator Murphy experienced a small hemorrhagic stroke."

"He has shown tremendous progress, and at the current rate of improvement, we expect that he will make a full recovery following rehabilitation," Suarez said.

Murphy had been in the hospital since having a stroke at his son's hockey game.

"Last Saturday afternoon, I felt something was not right with my body and called 9-1-1 out of an abundance of caution, and I'm glad I did," Murphy said in his statement. "I was diagnosed with a small bleed in the thalamus region of my brain."

Murphy's stroke came three days before Democratic candidates, in a stunning election result, won more than enough races to flip the Senate and House of Representatives from Republican majority control in January.

Murphy, who was not up for reelection this year, before the election had campaigned for Democratic candidates around the country, most recently in Michigan, Minnesota and Florida. He had been scheduled to campaign in New Hampshire on Sunday with Rep. Chris Pappas, who on Tuesday won election to the Senate.

Group-stage Cup games will be played throughout November, all counting in the regular-season standings. Quarter-finals will be played December 4-5 and semi-finals December 8-9 before the final on December 11 at Hinkle Fieldhouse in Indianapolis, Indiana.

#2980. OpenAI bugging suspect Alfozan pleads not guilty

The Saudi national charged with planting a listening device in the headquarters of OpenAI while he worked there pleaded not guilty on Friday during an appearance in San Francisco federal court.

Abdulrahman Alfozan, 35, was sent back to jail after the brief hearing. Alfozan has been held without bail since his arrest on a criminal complaint in early October.

He faces a maximum possible sentence of five years in prison if convicted in the case.

A grand jury indicted Alfozan on Oct. 27 on a single count of interception of oral communications, court records show.

Prosecutors say that he put a recording device in a television in an executive conference room at OpenAI's offices in San Francisco on Sept. 28.

Alfozan's lawyer declined to comment after the hearing before Magistrate Judge Peter Kang.

The judge ordered Alfozan to next appear in court on Nov. 17.

Alfozan is a venture partner in a Saudi Arabian venture capital firm, Raed Ventures.

"He currently serves as Engineering Lead at OpenAI, with a focus on AI Agents and the data platform," Alfozan's bio page on Raed's site says.

Prince Khalid Bin Bader Al Saud, a member of Saudi Arabia's royal family, is a venture partner in the same firm.

Alfozan's case comes as U.S. companies such as OpenAI are making rapid advancements in developing artificial intelligence agents, and as concerns grow about the potential theft of AI tech from those companies.In June, President Donald Trump, in an executive order on AI innovation, said it was U.S. policy "to protect American ingenuity and intellectual property from exploitation and theft by adversaries; and to cultivate America's advanced AI-enabled capabilities."Trump has had a close relationship with Saudi Arabia. But he has also taken steps during his second term in the White House to limit the number of visas for highly skilled foreign workers.OpenAI earlier this year held discussions with Saudi Arabia's Public Investment Fund, the country's sovereign wealth fund, about investing in the company.

— Jordan Novet reported from San Francisco

We're officially into Week 9 of the NFL season (and the looming NFL trade deadline), where teams will have to make a decision on the intended course of the remainder of their season — are they going all in? Or is it time to tear it all down and look toward the future?

NFL fans also await news on the fate of star running back Jonathan Taylor, who has been touted as a potential trade-deadline mover as the Indianapolis Colts kicked off their franchise reset with a fire sale. Follow along with Yahoo Sports for the latest NFL news, injury updates and more ahead of Week 9.

#2978. Mississippi Senate: Longshot Ty Pinkins says he aimed to spoil Hyde-Smith, boost Colom vote

Longshot Mississippi Senate candidate Ty Pinkins said that at first wanted to win — but that he stayed in to spoil.

Pinkins told a local TV station that after an April poll showed he had no chance of winning he remained in the race in order to boost Democratic nominee Scott Colom's chances of beating Republican incumbent Sen. Cindy Hyde-Smith.

Pinkins' purported strategy might pay off.

Colom is narrowly leading Hyde-Smith in ongoing counting by just 1,936 votes, a margin of just 0.2 percentage points, with 96.2% of the vote counted, according to the Associated Press. That margin is about one-tenth the size of Pinkins' share of the vote, 2.1 percentage points.

Pinkins has won just 20,010 votes so far out of the nearly 952,000 votes already counted.

"The only thing I did was stay in the race and let people shoot arrows at me," Pinkins said in an interview with WTVA in Mississippi on Thursday. "That is it. And that's a ... low price to pay to get someone in this race that I think can help people in the Delta and all across this state."

"The portion of the vote that I drew — and people were concerned about splitting the Black vote — what I actually did was split the Republican vote," he said.

Pinkins told the station in Tupelo that he intentionally avoided campaigning in areas with large Black populations to avoid cutting into the vote share of Colom, who, like Pinkins, is Black.

That tactic worked, he said, as the election results show he got most of his support from areas with relatively low Black populations.

"I'll be the boogeyman," he said. "But I'll tell you this: The independent in this race pulled most of his votes from a Republican, and for the first time since Reconstruction, a Democrat who is a Black man is leading."

Pinkins, a lawyer who previously ran for Mississippi secretary of state, was the Democratic nominee for Senate in 2024. He was crushed by Republican Sen. Roger Wicker, who won nearly 63% of the vote, compared with about 37% for Pinkins.

Despite that showing, the U.S. Army veteran decided to take another crack at the Senate in 2026.

But in the spring, he saw the writing on the wall for his candidacy.

"I got in this race to win," Pinkins told WTVA. "In April, I knew I was going to lose, but I also knew that I was pulling 70% of the Republican vote based on the [SPLC Action Fund and Impact Research] poll."

SPLC Action Fund, when it released the poll, said, "Sagging favorability numbers for Sen. Cindy Hyde-Smith could open up a path to victory for Democrats."

"Independent Ty Pinkins has the potential to play spoiler for either candidate," SPLC Action Fund said in April.

In the WTVA interview, Pinkins said, "I probably attended maybe, maybe six events over the summer" as part of his campaign.

"Six events. I don't have to," Pinkins said. "Why? I'm not going to win. So why go to these Black communities that he's going to?"

Pinkins said all he had to do was visit counties that have relatively low Black populations and "talk to some Republicans that said they're going to vote for me."

"They were literally calling me and sending me emails: 'We're going to vote for you because we don't want to waste our vote on Cindy Hyde-Smith, we don't want to hold our nose and vote for her, so we're going to vote for you. Please stay in the race,'" he said.

The campaigns of Colom and Hyde-Smith did not immediately respond to CNBC's requests for comment.

#2977. Kristaps Porziņģis announces he will ‘step away from the game I love’

The Kristaps Porziņģis saga has followed the Golden State Warriors around since the very first day of training camp. And while it seems like it will stay in the news cycle for quite a while, on Friday it took another step towards resolution, with the one-time All-Star posting a statement on social media, which seemed to suggest that he is retiring.

The statement, which he signed and labeled "official statement," reads in full as follows:

This season I was really looking forward to running it with this group, but as I ramped up, my body didn't respond the way I anticipated.

I'm doing good, but I know what it takes, and I'm not where I want to be to perform at the level that I expect from myself. At this point, I've made the decision to prioritize my health and step away from the game I love.

In this situation, I want to show the utmost respect for the Golden State Warriors and work with them to find a solution that makes sense.

I'm thankful to my family and close ones for always being there. Much appreciation to all of you for the support.

Porziņģis' statement is in line with all the reporting in recent days. Earlier today, our Marc Delucchi wrote about the reports that Porziņģis had played his last game with the team, and all that was left was to handle a trade for a player who was unlikely to ever play again. That came after news earlier in the week that Golden State was withholding payments towards Porziņģis' $20 million salary, and that neither Porziņģis nor the Players Association had plans to contest that.

So now what? It appears that Porziņģis is set to retire, but first wants to help out the Warriors. By remaining an active player — even an unpaid one — Porziņģis retains his cap hold which can be used to facilitate a trade. The Warriors can now attempt to trade Porziņģis' contract to a team in search of an expiring deal, and willing to offer a longer-term contract in exchange, such as the Milwaukee Bucks with Myles Turner, to name one of the many players the Dubs have been linked to.

After Porziņģis' contract is used to facilitate a trade — likely sooner rather than later — he can formally announce his retirement.

IOWA CITY — Saint Mary's transfer Drew McKeever is still yet to make his Iowa basketball debut.

He was unavailable for Iowa basketball's matchup with Eastern Illinois on Nov. 6, meaning that he missed the first two games of the 2026-27 season due to a back injury.

McKeever took part in pregame warmups against Lipscomb before ultimately being ruled out. But McKeever wasn't in uniform and did not participate in pregame warmups prior to the contest against Eastern Illinois.

He just kind of had some more spasms in his back a little bit yesterday. So, we're hopeful that it turns, but I think backs are tricky. One day he can feel good, and the next day, it's awful. Iowa's schedule is about to heat up. The Hawkeyes are now set to face three power-conference opponents in a row: Virginia Tech, Creighton and Xavier. That stretch begins with Virginia Tech on Nov. 10 in Sioux City.

#2975. Top Wall Street analysts are bullish on these 3 stocks for the long haul

The ongoing earnings season, Middle East tensions and buzz ahead of the U.S. midterm elections have been keeping investors busy. Further, worries about elevated artificial intelligence spending are weighing on investor sentiment.

Investors looking beyond the short-term noise and focusing on long-term returns can track top Wall Street analysts to pick the right stocks, backed by strong research.

Here are three stocks favored by some of Wall Street's top pros, according to TipRanks, a platform that ranks analysts based on their past performance.

This week's first stock pick is Datadog, an AI-powered observability and security platform. The company will announce its results for the third quarter on Nov. 4. Datadog is seeing strong demand for its offerings, thanks to cloud migration and rapid AI adoption. TipRanks' KPIs tool for DDOG shows the growth in its customer base over the past few years amid increasing market penetration and growing adoption of its products.

Heading into Q3 FY26 earnings, Bank of America analyst Koji Ikeda reiterated a buy rating on Datadog stock and raised his price target to $335 from $305. The analyst noted that industry checks and data indicate that infrastructure software demand is strengthening, with AI increasingly driving usage and growth.

Ikeda called Datadog and Atlassian his top picks, saying that companies that can show that AI is a tailwind to growth could see their valuation rerate, even at premium multiples. He added that the total addressable market for observability is growing faster than anticipated, driven by accelerated growth of enterprise AI and the rapid ramp of consumer AI agents.

The five-star analyst believes that focus will be on revenue growth, which he estimates to be 28.7% for Q3 FY26 versus the Street's consensus of 29.1%. Ikeda thinks that the market is underestimating the growth prospects of DDOG's security products. Meanwhile, he cautioned investors about concentration risk related to large customers.

"We think results will be supportive of our long-term thesis that Datadog is a strong share gainer in the rapidly expanding observability opportunity," said Ikeda.

Ikeda ranks No. 673 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been successful 55% of the time, delivering an average return of 10.9%. See Datadog Statistics on TipRanks.

The next pick, semiconductor giant Nvidia, is also benefiting from AI-driven demand for its products. After attending Nvidia's GTC Berlin conference, Morgan Stanley analyst Joseph Moore reaffirmed a buy rating on NVDA stock with a price target of $300, calling it a top pick in the semiconductor space.

The five-star analyst said that the conference supported his thesis that Nvidia continues to effectively navigate bottlenecks in the supply chain, land/power/shell, and funding. In fact, Moore is "convinced that all 3 aspects are opportunities for NVDA."

While Nvidia's GTC Berlin offered no major updates, Moore noted the conference reflected that the company continues to add to its significant advantages heading into 2027. The analyst said that Vera Rubin is gaining momentum, though details on next year's Rubin Ultra were lighter than expected.

With demand outpacing supply, Moore said that focus is on closing the gap. In this regard, he highlighted the supply chain deals on substrate, packaging and testing. While the analyst continues to view TSMC 3 nm wafer supply as the biggest challenge, he expects it to ease over time.

Moore ranks No. 142 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 59% of the time, delivering an average return of 23.4%. See Nvidia Ownership Structure on TipRanks.

Aerospace company SpaceX recently announced solid third-quarter results, with its AI revenue surging to $7.56 billion from $813 million in the prior-year quarter.

Impressed by the results, Needham analyst Ryan Koontz reiterated a buy rating on SPCX stock with a price target of $250. Koontz highlighted that SpaceX's Q3 results significantly exceeded expectations, proving that the company's vertically integrated business model is driving massive scale.

Furthermore, Koontz emphasized the 195.2% sequential growth in SpaceX's AI revenue, fueled by wholesale spot compute deals. He noted management's increasing focus on its AI strategy, given that it is optimistic about adding 10 gigawatts of incremental capacity during 2027.

"With increasing confidence on Starship re-use, we meaningfully increase our estimates," said Koontz.

Koontz ranks No. 357 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 42% of the time, delivering an average return of 23.7%. See SpaceX Technical Analysis on TipRanks.

#2972. Trump says Stephen Miller to replace Susie Wiles as White House chief of staff

President Donald Trump on Friday announced White House deputy chief of staff Stephen Miller will be promoted to chief of staff, replacing Susie Wiles.

Trump revealed the stunning shake-up just three days after Democrats won a landslide victory in the midterm elections, gaining majority control in the House and Senate and scrambling the trajectory of Trump's last two years in office.

Elevating Miller, an architect of Trump's controversial mass-deportation efforts, is bound to raise the hackles of the incoming class of congressional Democrats, who have already vowed to investigate the government's immigration policies.

"It has been the privilege of my lifetime to have served on his team from Day One, and I am honored beyond words to serve as his White House Chief of Staff," Miller said on X later Friday.

"The mission now is more clear than ever: Fight with all of our soul to support our President and defend this magnificent Republic," he said.

Miller will take over starting Monday, Trump said in a Truth Social post, praising his longtime aide as "one of my most trusted advisors."

Wiles, "who has done an outstanding job, will be moving over to the Trump Political Operation," the president wrote.

Asked about the move later Friday afternoon, Trump said, "I wanted to make a change."

"I think Susie's done a very good job, great job. But I just wanted to make it," Trump told reporters as he left the White House for an event in Cape Canaveral, Florida.

"Stephen's been great," Trump said, adding, "Sometimes in life you have to make moves."

Wiles resigned Thursday night, a senior White House source told MS NOW. Wiles told Trump, who was said to be angry with her following the GOP's huge election losses, "let me make this easy on you — I am going to resign," MS NOW reported.

"Today is my last day as White House Chief of Staff," Wiles said in an X post Friday afternoon. "Serving President Trump in the White House has been the honor of a lifetime."

"While my role may be changing, my commitment to the President and the mission never will," she wrote.

Senior administration officials were quick to congratulate Miller.

"Patriots in control," read an X post from Defense Secretary Pete Hegseth, whose job security has come into question after some Republicans, including Vice President JD Vance, blamed the midterm results on the Iran war.

Vance also praised Miller on X, saying he "will do great things."

The official X account for Trump's political operation celebrated the announcement by posting a black-and-white photo of Miller under the caption, "It's Miller time."

Democrats and other critics, meanwhile, filed in to denounce the pick.

House Minority Leader Hakeem Jeffries, D-N.Y., who could be elected House speaker next year, called Miller "a dangerous xenophobe and reckless extremist who has American blood on his hands."

"His presence in government should be eradicated like a malignant tumor. Fire him today," Jeffries wrote on X.

Senate Minority Leader Chuck Schumer, D-N.Y., wrote that Trump "learned nothing from his historic defeat."

"Stephen Miller is the architect of the most barbaric, inhumane policies of this administration. Trump couldn't have made a worse choice. Our Democratic majorities will fight him every step of the way," Schumer wrote.

Others took Miller's promotion as a sign that Trump, who seemed to express some willingness to negotiate with a blue Congress, will instead remain combative.

"For those who hoped the @POTUS might pivot to work with the incoming Congress, the appointment of Miller is a bucket of ICE cold water," wrote David Axelrod, a former senior advisor to President Barack Obama.

The White House reshuffle follows multiple resignations of senior staff in the Department of Justice since Election Day. They include Harmeet Dhillon, a leader in the DOJ's civil rights division, John Eisenberg, who headed the national security division, and Jason Reding Quiñones, who had been in charge of conspiracy investigations related to Trump's foes.

Wiles oversaw Trump's winning 2024 presidential campaign and has wielded major influence in his second administration, while largely staying out of the spotlight. She drew focus in late 2025, when Vanity Fair reported that Wiles admitted that Trump had pursued "retribution" as president and that he had an "alcoholic's personality."

Trump, and many other top administration officials, defended Wiles at the time, underscoring her status in the White House.

While Wiles is not fully leaving Trump's orbit, her resignation follows a pattern of other top female officials who have left or been fired from Trump's second administration, and then replaced by men.

76ers star LeBron James breaks 396-game streak in NBA Cup loss to Cavs appeared first on ClutchPoints. Add ClutchPoints as a Preferred Source by clicking here.

The Philadelphia 76ers' hot winning streak came to an end on Friday following a disappointing 119-95 loss to the Cleveland Cavaliers. The loss also marked an unfortunate night for LeBron James, as the NBA legend saw a remarkable personal streak come to an end on the Cavaliers' home floor.

According to ESPN's Dave McMenamin, the performance ended an extraordinary run of consecutive double-digit scoring games for James in Cleveland. The longtime NBA superstar had maintained that streak for 396 regular-season appearances before Friday's disappointing NBA Cup outing.

"Tonight broke a streak of 396 straight regular season games in double-digit scoring in Cleveland for James. It was also his third game vs. CLE in the calendar year with 5 or fewer FGM and 5 or more turnovers," McMenamin wrote.

#2970. Santoli: AI bull market almost surely needs tech stocks to recover to keep advancing

The road to bull-market glory does not run through Walmart and Berkshire Hathaway and Eli Lilly.

There's been a virtual buying panic in traditionally defensive, high-quality stocks over the past few weeks, a near-equal-and-opposite offset to the selling pressure in semiconductors and AI-build-out spenders. The three stocks above — the only members of the $1 trillion market-cap club not in Big Tech – have collectively added $550 billion in market value in the past month, almost making up for what Alphabet has lost.

The Nasdaq-100 and the S&P 500 Low Volatility ETF (SPLV) are rushing toward one another in a satisfying picture of symmetry that nonetheless reflects an almost comical level of tactical rotation executed by an investment community accustomed to playing one factor against another.

While this to-and-fro has so far protected the S&P 500 as a whole from an appreciable setback for months, it has left it stymied around levels that date back to May — before we got two earnings-reporting seasons showing 30% annual profit growth.

A week ago here, I suggested the AI theme was nearing a make-or-break moment to stage a recovery or risk surrendering its leadership status and destabilizing the broader market. Thus far, this trade has failed to respond to moderate oversold conditions or a run of cosmetically strong hyperscaler quarterly results, further loosening its grip on the benefit of the doubt.

In stressed markets undergoing soul-searching about the long-term bullish thesis, prices often need to travel further to find buyers with conviction while the market action must disappoint dip-buyers to extinguish complacency. Hard to know if we're there, but presumably we're now closer.

The Nasdaq-100 closed Monday right at its 200-day moving average, off almost 11% from its record high. After an enervating failed Monday-morning bounce attempt, the Philadelphia Semiconductor Index finished down another 3.8% and settled under the late-July low that was set as the Situational Awareness hedge fund was finished liquidating.

"Markets did not get the memo that bearish seasonal pressure typically subsides in mid-October and instead ended the month with some of the most acute selling pressure that has been realized during the late-summer/early-fall period," notes JPMorgan technical strategist Jason Hunter. He's advising clients to remain on the defensive "until we see clear evidence of a bottoming process on the charts or until there are clear signs of capitulation."

This is a time-tested playbook, though many a sharp market pullback has ended without what would qualify as unqualified capitulation.

The market action is winning some plaudits for the way non-tech stocks have held up better, many groups benefiting from the "quality" rotation while abandoned consumer cyclicals get a reprieve from easing oil prices and the slide in the 10-year Treasury yield back below 5%.

This gives active managers plenty to do in playing against a highly concentrated and tech-dependent S&P 500. But I'll continue to argue that the bull market built on the AI story will almost surely need the AI story to get back on track to keep advancing. A prolonged period when the equal-weight S&P 500 outperforms the market-cap-weighted version is otherwise known – usually – as a bear market.

The AI-related stocks' weighting in the S&P 500 is off the highs but remains near 45%, according to Morgan Stanley.

As the chart shows, AI has wobbled relative to the rest of the market during this uptrend before reasserting itself.  But there's a trickier element to the current gut check: In prior phases of the buildout, the key limiting bottleneck gave investors something to buy in order to exploit it.

The initial scarcity of GPUs meant to own Nvidia above all else from 2023 through 2024. When cloud capacity, power and cooling were found to be the crucial scarce resources, hyperscaler stocks worked and the AI-adjacent industrials were drastically revalued. Then memory emerged as the chokepoint and Micron and Sandisk went stratospheric.

Now, as Nvidia's Jensen Huang has said, capital is the constraint and that not only leaves no asset to buy, but it raises the cost of capital broadly as the mega-caps go free-cash-flow-negative.

Overlapping feedback loops and scrambled correlations across the markets and the real economy have further sapped investor conviction. Treasury yields no longer move with oil but largely with AI-capex sentiment, with both in retreat lately. AI-stock setbacks also mute inflation momentum and the equity wealth effect, meaning fewer Federal Reserve rate hikes are anticipated now versus a month ago.

This all probably sounds like an elaborate trap, and it has become one for the previously crowded trades. Could this reflect the end game for the AI-exuberance market chapter, which as I keep pointing out peaked around the SpaceX IPO in June? Impossible to say for sure, but if it were, I'm not sure things would look much different to this point.

But I don't want to dismiss the possibility that a lot of the known challenges are largely reflected in prices.

I argued in August that we should assume the valuation peak for this cycle was in. We've now retrenched to a forward P/E of 18.5 on the S&P 500 from 23 a year ago and the Nasdaq-100 at 20x is at the slimmest premium to the S&P 500 valuation in nine years. I'm on board with the idea that the market is telling us companies are now "over-earning," but if nothing else there is less valuation risk now than in recent months.

Sentiment toward AI stocks is in the dumps. Funds' positioning in tech has retreated below neutral. Important trading lows this year have happened near the turn of the month on March 30 and July 29, and the Nasdaq-100's relative-strength reading is exactly as oversold as it was at those turns.

So, stay alert for possible relief bounces that could evolve into something more consequential if indeed the midterm elections pass in a way that allows traders to unclench a bit into the year's home stretch.

John Kolovos of Macro Risk Advisors on the latest reading: "Similar to valuation, sentiment is not a timing tool, but it is getting harder and harder to find any bulls, as a near-perfect storm of tight financial conditions is being met with tech earnings that have gone from bad to worse in the blink of an eye."

-Another good big-picture conversation on the practical business and investment implications of the next phase of AI proliferation on Patrick O'Shaughnessy's Invest Like the Best podcast, this one with investor Cliff Sosin. His comparison of AI infrastructure economics to internet connectivity providers and his argument for how most of the productivity boost will go toward "consumer surplus" rather than corporate profits are particularly interesting.

-For my birthday Monday, my colleague and friend Sara Eisen wondered how I would write a birthday message to myself. I immediately regretted suggesting she ask ChatGPT, which generated this credible and somewhat embarrassing result:

"Another year older, though the longer-term trend remains constructive. Some modest deterioration in the underlying internals, perhaps, but nothing that suggests a decisive breakdown.

"The candles on the cake are approaching historically elevated levels, though valuation concerns have persisted for years without derailing the advance. At this stage, the key question isn't whether I've gotten older — that's largely priced in — but whether the wisdom gains can continue to offset the inevitable compression in energy multiples.

"For now, I'm maintaining a cautiously optimistic outlook, with upside optionality, downside support from family and friends, and no immediate need for Fed intervention. Happy birthday to me. The secular bull market remains intact."

The stampede into perceived defensive stocks has been a crucial driver of Apple shares' massive outperformance over the Nasdaq-100 since the AI-capex agita flared up months ago. Apple heading into Monday's earnings report had outperformed the Nasdaq-100 by nearly 20 percentage points over the prior six months.

Its singular refusal to join the aggressive spending binge to build AI capacity preserved its free cash flow and made it the perfect counterweight to the capex trade, while inflating Apple's valuation to a 15-year high.

But its advantage in free-cash-flow yield has now narrowed, thanks to its stock-price gains and the collapse of peer shares.

#2969. Investors brace for lower Anthropic valuation, calling $1.5 trillion a 'stretch'

Following tech's selloff on Wall Street this week, some investors are lowering their expectations for Anthropic.

The artificial intelligence company had been looking toward a $2 trillion valuation in its IPO up until recently, but stakeholders told CNBC that even $1.5 trillion is now in doubt, with one investor calling that number a "stretch."

The change in sentiment comes alongside growing concerns about the outsized spending by AI companies and a sharp post-midterm slump in the market. The Nasdaq plummeted more than 5% on Thursday, its steepest drop in 19 months, reflecting fears that Democrats' newfound power in Washington, D.C., and a nationwide rebuke of AI will lead to a slowdown in the buildout of data centers.

Bearishness on the AI trade was on the rise before the election. The Nasdaq suffered its worst month in October since March 2025, led by declines in chipmakers and neoclouds.

Anthropic is set to list on the Nasdaq, under the ticker ANT, and is gearing up for an expected debut in a matter of weeks. Last month, Anthropic was reportedly seeking a valuation as high as $2 trillion. As of May, the company was privately valued at $965 billion.

With the latest market turmoil, one investor said $1 trillion to $1.25 trillion is a more likely valuation target, although that could change with market conditions. The investors asked not to be named because they're not authorized to speak about the matter.

Gil Luria, an analyst at D.A. Davidson, told CNBC that, "Investors will expect the IPO to be priced at least at the low end of previous expectations."

"The market really needs this IPO to go well and the bankers on the deal surely understand that," Luria said. "The best way to set Anthropic up for success is to price the deal well."

The company is expected to kick off its roadshow next week.
