#2965. MLB announces Pete Crow-Armstrong, Cody Bellinger as Platinum Glove winners in Friday night news dump

Pete Crow-Armstrong and Cody Bellinger have won MLB's Platinum Glove awards, meant to honor the top overall defender in each league. It's unclear if the league actually wants people to know that.

For reasons that remain clear only to MLB, the 2026 Platinum Gloves were announced at 11 p.m. ET on a Friday night, with the Chicago Cubs and New York Yankees taking home an award that is decided by an easily swayed fan vote. It is the first time either player has won the award.

The pair seems happy with their new hardware.

The Platinum Gloves are selected from among the 10 Gold Glovers in each league. This year, those were:

Pitcher: Tanner Bibee, Cleveland Guardians

First base: Christian Walker, Houston Astros

Second base: Cole Young, Seattle Mariners

Left field: Cody Bellinger, New York Yankees

Center field: Ceddane Rafaela, Boston Red Sox

Right field: Wilyer Abreu, Boston Red Sox

Catcher: Gabriel Moreno, Arizona Diamondbacks

Second base: Brice Turang, Milwaukee Brewers

Shortstop: Geraldo Perdomo, Arizona Diamondbacks

Left field: Jackson Chourio, Milwaukee Brewers

Center field: Pete Crow-Armstrong, Chicago Cubs

Right field: Corbin Carroll, Arizona Diamondbacks

The timing of the announcement was nothing new, nor was the methodology. The winner is decided by combining a fan vote held by Rawlings with "votes" from a stat called the SABR Defensive Index (SDI).

Bellinger led the AL in SDI, while Crow-Armstrong ranked second behind only Milwaukee Brewers second baseman Brice Turang, another Gold Glover. As for other advanced defensivee stats, Crow-Armstrong ranked first in his league in Statcast's Fielding Run Value and third in Defensive Runs Saved. Bellinger was 21st and second in those stats, respectively.

It is, of course, extremely difficult to compare defenders across different positions, as these stats are trying to figure out how, say, an outfielder with a penchant for difficult catches compares to a catcher who can throw out runners and frame pitches. It's an inherent flaw of the Platinum Gloves, which would be more of a problem if anyone took them seriously.

At the very least, the Platinum Glove is another feather in the cap this season for Crow-Armstrong, who is widely expected to win NL MVP next week. The Cubs star posted MLB's seventh 40-40 season this year, making him an elite player on both offense and defense this season.

Bellinger, meanwhile, received his first All-Star nod since winning NL MVP in 2019 and was among the Yankees' top players this season after returning to the team last winter on a five-year, $162.5 million contract.

Rawling also announced the Toronto Blue Jays and Chicago Cubs as the winners of the Team Gold Gloves. The Cubs ranked third in MLB this season in defensive efficiency (i.e. the rate at which they turned balls in play into outs), while the Blue Jays were 13th. Toronto, which had no Gold Glovers, also led the AL in errors.

#2963. Kristaps Porziņģis says he will 'step away' from basketball in wake of lingering health issues

Kristaps Porziņģis is stepping away from the NBA while dealing with ongoing health issues, the veteran center announced Friday.

In a statement on social media, Porziņģis said he was not able to play at the level he hoped and has decided to focus on his health.

"This season I was really looking forward to running it with this group, but as I ramped up, my body didn't respond the way I anticipated," Porziņģis wrote. "I'm doing good, but I know what it takes, and I'm not where I want to be to perform at the level that I expect from myself. At this point, I've made the decision to prioritize my health and step away from the game I love."

Porzingis, who signed a two-year, $40 million contract with the Golden State Warriors this summer, said he hopes to work with the Warriors to "find a solution that makes sense" going forward. The center has been indefinitely away from the team this season while dealing with his health.

ESPN reported Wednesday the Warriors have not paid Porzingis any of his $20 million annual salary while he contemplated his NBA future. Porzingis has reportedly not contested the lack of payments.

Porzingis was acquired by the Warriors before the trade deadline last February and signed the extension to stay in Golden State in June. However, a personal "health issue" caused him to miss the start of training camp.

Neither Porzingis nor the Warriors has clarified what the center has been dealing with. But Porzingis has been open about his struggles with postural orthostatic tachycardia syndrome (POTS), a chronic condition that can affect the nervous system. Last fall, Porzingis revealed his POTS symptoms flared up during the 2025 NBA playoffs, when he was with the Boston Celtics, and severely limited his ability to play.

Porzingis said last month he was "getting better and better" after consulting with doctors, though he called the health issues "frustrating." It is unclear if Friday's announcement is a full retirement, or just a pause in his NBA career.

Nicknamed "The Unicorn" for his unique big-man skillset, the 31-year-old Latvian center steps away from basketball after an 11-year career spread across six teams. Porzingis is a 2024 NBA champion with the Celtics and earned an All-Star nod in 2018.

#2962. A disturbing employment trend is hiding beneath the latest strong job numbers

Americans who lose their jobs these days are facing a harsh new problem: It's getting harder to find another one quickly.

The average jobless worker's bout of unemployment surpassed 27 weeks in October on a seasonally adjusted basis, according to Friday's jobs report from the Bureau of Labor Statistics. That amounts to a job search of nearly seven months, the longest outside of the pandemic period since 2016, when the U.S. labor market was finally getting back on track after the 2008 financial crisis.

About 2 million Americans have been without work for at least 27 weeks, the BLS reported. People in this category are referred to as the "long-term unemployed" and accounted for 27.6% of all jobless workers in October, the highest share since late 2021.

"This is a series that can be noisy from month to month," said Cory Stahle, senior economist at Indeed, a hiring platform. "But if you look over a longer time horizon, there is no mistake in the trend."

It's a troubling sign in an otherwise steady labor market. Workers stuck on the sidelines are a casualty of what's become a "low-hire, low-fire" job market, in which companies are hesitant to lay workers off, but aren't adding them either.

Research shows that Americans who experience long-term unemployment face depleted savings, lower earnings potential over time and declining mental health.

What's more, workers who are unemployed for extended periods risk falling into a self-perpetuating cycle, researchers at the Federal Reserve Bank of Richmond wrote earlier this year.

"Time spent unemployed may itself reduce the probability of finding a job, perhaps because skills depreciate, networks thin or employers interpret long unemployment durations as a negative signal," the researchers warned.

Workers stop being able to claim most, if not all, government unemployment benefits after 26 weeks in a year. Given that, a rising share of long-term unemployed people can explain in part why initial and continuing jobless claims have remained low.

"Now, the average unemployed person takes longer to find a job than their benefits would last," Stahle said. "That is a really key point of this."

While jobless workers appear to be having a tough time, this week's employment data shows strength in other areas of the labor market.

Nonfarm payrolls blew past economists' forecasts for October and added 165,000 jobs, according to Friday's release. The unemployment rate ticked down to 4.1%, slightly lower than economists predicted.

Job openings came in much higher than economists expected in September, according to a separate report from the BLS released on Tuesday. The number of workers quitting jobs — a barometer of their confidence to obtain new employment — spiked to levels not seen since March 2025.

The BLS also said layoffs and discharges dropped to their lowest volume in more than a year. However, a recent string of high-profile workforce cuts could throw cold water on views among workers that the market is heating up.

CNBC reported this week that Disney is planning to axe thousands of jobs, the latest of several waves of layoffs at the media giant this year. American Express said last month that it was slashing head count by 5% as it reorients around artificial intelligence.

"It's difficult to separate what's happening to you or the people around you from the big picture," said Elizabeth Renter, lead economist at Life360. "When your LinkedIn is filled with layoffs and your social circle features a few folks who have been job seeking for several months, positive narratives about the labor market can seem jarring."

#2961. NFL predictions: 3 bold takes on the Cowboys and Colts game

The Dallas Cowboys, for just a moment, looked like they had righted their season after beating the Philadelphia Eagles and were on their way to taking out the Arizona Cardinals. You probably don't need me to tell you how that turned out.

In similar fashion, I got crushed on my three bold takes for last week's game. I vow to bounce back this week, just like the Cowboys will hopefully do.

The Cowboys are on the road for this week's game against the Indianapolis Colts, and are 5.5-point favorites, according to our friends at FanDuel. The over/under is 51.5 points. The Cowboys being favored is more an indictment of the Colts than anything else.

On to the bold predictions for the Cowboys versus Colts.

The headline says Colts QBs for two reasons. One is Daniel Jones, and his backup Anthony Richardson, have wheels, and it feels likely the Colts may try to pull a "Cardinals switcheroo" on the Cowboys if Jones gets off to a rough start. So both Jones and Richardson could get reps in the game.

Secondly, the Cowboys defense is never able to contain mobile quarterbacks. It's just not in their DNA. So Jones alone, or maybe in combination with Richardson, will go for over 75 yards on the ground.

There is no real scheme-related thing for this bold prediction. Just a feeling. Cowboys fandom is frustrated with how Brian Schottenheiemer is using Pickens. The receiver himself is likely getting frustrated at the lack of action. Trade rumors have been attached to his name.

That's a lot of noise. To quiet it all down, the Cowboys will feature Pickens more, and one of those targets will pay off big with a 40+ yard touchdown.

Everybody is down on DaRon Bland. The ballhawk CB from years ago has disappeared. Even his coach, Christian Parker, has come out and said the team needs more from Bland.

In this game, Bland gets himself a little redemption by picking off a pass. If we were really bold, we'd say he not only picks it off, but takes it to the house for a pick-six, just like the old days.

#2960. 'I'd be stunned' if Philip Rivers isn't NC State's next coach, Alabama sports reporter tells ABC11

A longtime, award-winning Alabama sports reporter who has closely covered Philip Rivers' high school coaching career says he would be "stunned" if the former NC State quarterback does not become the Wolfpack's next head coach, with timing appearing to be the biggest remaining question.

Ben Thomas of AL.com has covered Rivers since his arrival at St. Michael Catholic in 2021 as head football coach. He discussed Rivers' future during an interview with ABC11's Travon Miles as speculation continues to grow around the former NFL quarterback potentially returning to Raleigh.

"I think for the last couple of weeks, I've thought Philip Rivers is going to be the coach at North Carolina State at some point," Thomas said. "I mean, I think the question right now is when does that happen? When is our announcement made?"

WATCH: Philip Rivers speaks after high school team's season ends

Speculation about an announcement from NC State on its next football coach will ramp up after Philip Rivers' team was ousted Friday night in the state playoffs.

Thomas said Rivers met with NC State officials earlier in the week in Fairhope, where Rivers coaches the Cardinal.

"I'd be surprised if he's not the coach at NC State; I'm going to be stunned," Thomas said. "I think the timing, the question now is what's the timing."

Thomas said he first began to believe Rivers could be a serious candidate as soon as NC State moved on from former coach Dave Doeren.

Thomas said he had heard Rivers had withdrawn from serving as offensive coordinator for an Alabama all-star game scheduled for December. Shortly afterward, the NC State vacancy emerged.

"When I heard that Philip pulled out of that game and then a couple of days later, there was an opening at North Carolina State, that seemed that the pieces kind of seemed to add up," Thomas said.

While Rivers has publicly confirmed speaking with NC State, Thomas said the former quarterback has remained largely consistent in his approach.

"He did say a lot of nice things about how that place is different," Thomas said. "Obviously, love that place, you know, nothing's imminent, and we'll see where we go from here."

Rivers has said previously that he intended to coach his high school team through the postseason. That ended for St. Michael on Friday night when the Cardinal lost 42-35 in the playoffs to Glenwood School.

With his team's ouster, speculation is certain to ramp up on when or if an announcement could come from NC State.

"We'll see what the future holds," Rivers said after Friday night's game. He again confirmed that he met with NC State officials on Wednesday, but said it was just a step in the process.

Thomas said Rivers originally viewed high school coaching as his long-term goal after retiring from professional football. However, he said he believes Rivers' brief NFL return last year and subsequent head coaching interview with the Buffalo Bills helped broaden his coaching ambitions.

"Being a high school coach was not a publicity stunt for him," Thomas said. "That was his dream."

According to Thomas, Rivers later realized, "Something I know now is I can impact people, football players at any level, not just at the high school level."

Thomas also pointed to the role of Rivers' son, Gunner, who is committed to NC State.

"I think that's a huge part of it," Thomas said.

Asked whether the chance to coach his son in college was a factor in his decision, Rivers responded, "Oh, sure."

Rivers said he wouldn't call that "the factor, the deciding factor, but certainly, it would be special."

Thomas suggested the situation could be different if Gunner were committed elsewhere.

"If he was committed to Auburn or Alabama or somewhere, we'd be talking about a lot different situation," he said.

Having watched Rivers build St. Michael's football program, Thomas said the former NFL star possesses the energy, leadership style and competitiveness that have made him successful as a coach.

"He's so energetic," Thomas said, recalling Rivers' interactions with players. "He reminds the guys all the time that, hey, enjoy this."

Thomas also highlighted Rivers' ability to attract players and supporters.

"What the high school coaches have told me, and people know him say, is he'll be able to galvanize that fan base," Thomas said. "People want to contribute to that program."

Thomas added, "People want to come play football for Philip Rivers."

As for concerns about Rivers making the jump directly from high school football to a major college program, Thomas said he believes Rivers' leadership qualities and football background would translate.

"I think people want to play for him, and I think he's got the football knowledge and the competitive fire to be successful wherever he is," Thomas said.

Although Thomas acknowledged family considerations remain significant for Rivers, who has a large family and established roots in Alabama, he repeatedly returned to the same conclusion.

"I would be shocked if it didn't go that way at this point," Thomas said.

#2959. Fannie Mae, Freddie Mac rally as Ackman presses for conservatorship exit

The midterm elections could be a turning point for the release of Fannie Mae and Freddie Mac from government control, according to billionaire investor Bill Ackman.

Investors may agree with him, if the rally in both stocks is any sign.

The Federal National Mortgage Association, known as Fannie Mae, and Federal Home Loan Mortgage Corporation, or Freddie Mac, have each jumped more than 60% from Oct. 14 to Monday's close. The stocks were both up roughly 3% on Tuesday.

Ackman, the founder of Pershing Square Capital Management, has long pushed for the two entities to be released from federal conservatorship.

"The reason why it's not being touched right now is, you know, midterms," he said in an interview with CNBC's "Squawk Box."

Ackman, who owns both names through Pershing Square, believes Freddie and Fannie have the potential to increase "10-fold from current share prices," he said in his quarterly letter to shareholders, issued on Monday.

Fannie and Freddie buy mortgages from banks and other lenders, and then package them into mortgage-backed securities, known as MBS. Their operations are critical to the housing market, providing liquidity to banks and creating a secondary market for MBS.

The entities were placed into conservatorship in September 2008, during the financial crisis. The stocks were delisted from the New York Stock Exchange in 2010 and have traded over the counter since then.

Ackman wants the conservatorship to end, arguing the entities would be worth multiples of their current valuation if they were freed from government control.

President Donald Trump has supported ending the conservatorship of Fannie and Freddie. But it remains politically and economically contentious.

The entities operate under strict capital threshold requirements and would need to be recapitalized, which could happen through issuing common equity. However, the Treasury Department would need to figure out an exit plan for its roughly $388 billion ownership stake in the entities through preferred securities and warrants.

Congress may also need to play a role, passing legislation to restructure the government's role in mortgage financing and home-loan guarantees.

Nonetheless, Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent recently said it could happen before Trump's term ends, telling 2Way's "The Morning Meeting" podcast that the administration will tackle the ownership structure "next year, the year after." He said affordability will be guiding the decision.

One point of controversy is whether ending the conservatorship of Fannie and Freddie would increase the spreads on MBS over Treasuries, effectively making mortgages costlier. Bessent has sought to reassure the market that wouldn't happen.

"Whatever we do is going to be guided by making sure that the spread on [agency] mortgages … over the Treasury does not increase," Bessent said.

Ackman argues that privatizing the GSEs would help insulate them from political meddling. "Fannie and Freddie are these massive, enormously profitable institutions where whichever political party controls them…if they're still in conservatorship, can influence the outcome of elections," he told CNBC.

In one scenario, he explained, Democrats win the White House, conservatorship is still in place, and Democrats push Fannie and Freddie to lower credit standards in order to expand home ownership and affordability.

"If they're going to move more aggressively into borrowers with lower credit quality, for example, to achieve a political objective, that's going to have an impact on trillions of dollars outstanding mortgage securities," Ackman said. "It's going to have an impact on overall credit spreads."

MBS investors want certainty about how the entities will be run, he noted.

"That only gets resolved in a world in which they exit conservatorship, they become publicly traded companies that are owned by institutional and retail investors," Ackman said. "We set the rules. In conservatorship, the president can do anything."

#2957. Warriors' Kristaps Porzingis issues update on his health, NBA future

Golden State Warriors center Kristaps Porziņģis, who has not been with the team as he addresses long-term health concerns, announced he will step away from basketball.

"This season I was really looking forward to running it with this group, but as I ramped up, my body didn't respond the way I anticipated," Porziņģis wrote Friday, Nov. 6 in a statement posted to social media.

"I'm doing good, but I know what it takes, and I'm not where I want to be to perform at the level that I expect from myself. At this point, I've made the decision to prioritize my health and step away from the game I love."

It's worth noting that Porziņģis didn't outright mention retirement in the statement, which could potentially leave a return to the sport down the road, should his health improve.

Porziņģis, 31, has been dealing with an undisclosed illness that has prevented him from joining the Warriors. He had missed all of training camp in late September and has been away from the team. Warriors coach Steve Kerr said last month that Porziņģis would be in the team facility for a check-in with Golden State's sports medicine and performance team.

Previously, stretching back to Porziņģis' final season with the Celtics in 2024-25, he had expressed frustrations over repeated absences that were caused from an undiagnosed condition that prevented him from having the energy and stamina to play.

Eventually, Porziņģis disclosed that he was dealing with postural orthostatic tachycardia syndrome (POTS) autoimmune symptoms, though he has not offered specific updates in recent months.

The Celtics traded Porziņģis to the Hawks in June 2025. Just eight months later, Atlanta then shipped Porziņģis in a separate deal to the Warriors, where he played only 15 games for the team.

ESPN reported Wednesday, Nov. 4 that Porziņģis was not being paid his $20 million salary for failing to render services to the team.

A one-time All-Star, Porziņģis is known for stretching the floor with his shooting range, while providing a rim-protecting presence on the other end. Across his 10-year career, Porziņģis has averaged 19.5 points, 7.6 rebounds and 1.9 assists per game in 533 appearances.

"In this situation, I want to show the utmost respect for the Golden State Warriors and work with them to find a solution that makes sense," Porziņģis continued in his statement. "I'm thankful to my family and close ones for always being there. Much appreciation to all of you for the support. Onwards and upwards."

#2956. Raiders Week 9: 49ers explosive offense, stingy defense are imposing

At 6-1 overall, it's no surprise the San Francisco 49ers boast a Top 10 offense and defense at this particular juncture of the 2026 season.

The Niners have the fourth-best scoring offense of the 32 NFL teams at 197 points per game and accumulated the sixth-highest total yards at 2,600. In addition, the team's defense allows the sixth-least amount of points at 139 and the fourth-least amount of yards at 2,095.

The Las Vegas Raiders' Klint Kubiak and Rob Leonard will certainly have their hands full when the Silver & Black head to Santa Clara for a Week 9 matchup with the 49ers. The head coach and defensive coordinator, respectively, are the play callers for offense and defense and have the headset and play sheet to combat their counterparts in San Francisco head coach Kyle Shanahan and defensive coordinator Raheem Morris.

Of the Raiders combo, Leonard has the tougher challenge trying to stymie Shanahan's explosive offense that hasn't skipped a beat even though injuries change the lineup.

"Yeah, they do. They're fundamentally sound, man," Leonard said of the 49ers offense. "They're a clean operation, and they got some good players. Quarterback's playing at a high level, good tight end, running backs, and the offensive line is really good. So, that's what jumps out to me, how fundamentally sound they are, and they're able to put a lot of pressure on you."

With an accurate and collected quarterback in Brock Purdy, Shanahan funnels the offense through his signal caller and it's one that attacks every level of the opposing defense. Very physical in the trenches, the offensive line buys Purdy the time to go through his progressions and get the ball to a target like tight end George Kittle. The 33-year-old veteran leads the team with 34 catches for 425 yards and five touchdowns and shows no signs of slowing down — as evidence by his 56-yard catch-and-run below:

Shanahan's ground game is domineering ranking eighth-best in yards (892) and third in rushing touchdowns (10) and bolsters his ability to open the full play book to deploy on the opponent. Christian McCaffrey paces the run game with 84 carries for 389 yards and eight touchdowns. Spell back Kaelon Black has 57 totes for 233 yards and a score while Purdy has scrambled for 161 yards and a touchdown on 32 attempts.

As fundamentally sound Leonard says the 49ers are on offense, his Raiders defense must be equally as coherent — something Las Vegas has tumbled down the rankings as it lost four-straight games before a Week 8 win against the New York Jets to get to 4-4 overall.

The Silver & Black are giving up the second-most points (230) and third-most yards (3,055). Leonard's unit is ranked 25th against the pass with 1,884 yards allowed and 31st against the run with 1,171 yards yielded. The defensive play caller knows his crew has a lot to work on and can't afford a lapse against a quality team like the Niners. Especially with San Francisco's penchant to run outside zone runs and a multitude of pre-snap motions to create chaos. To combat this, Leonard is likely to use more single-high safety alignments for run fits and creatively blitz to ground the 49ers.

Then — now don't gasp if this brings past pain with the Raiders — maintain eye discipline is vital. The motions Shanahan habitually uses is intended to break coverages that end up explosive plays. Las Vegas can ill-afford to get "caught looking" against San Francisco, a team that's deployed 21 personnel (two running backs and one tight end) to perfection at times this season.

How Leonard counters with personnel is going to be intriguing.

Perhaps going into sub packages built on speed and athleticism that brings slot corner Taron Johnson in the lineup is the route to go. Safeties Treydan Stukes and Jeremy Chinn have shown the ability to stop the run while Johnson is both a steady cover man and tackler over the middle.

"Stack plays," Leonard said when asked what was stressed during practice this week. "Really focusing on us, our alignment, our assignment, key technique, fundamentals, and it's especially that time of the year where it's hard to really out-scheme people. Everybody knows who they are, so you have to go out and execute."

The chess game between the Raiders offense and 49ers defense is the marquee matchup for the Silver & Black. Unlike the defense, Kubiak concocted the sixth-best scoring offense in the league (221 points) that's generated the 12th most yards (2,739). The aerial attack is third-best in the NFL with 2,121 yards and 19 touchdowns.

Morris, meanwhile, is the architect of Niners' sixth-ranked defense in points allowed (139) and fourth-ranked in yards allowed (2,095). Under Morris' command, San Francisco has the fifth-best run defense allowing 623 yards and the second-least rushing touchdowns (three).

With Las Vegas unable to get a consistent ground game going (31st in yards at 618), expect Kubiak to dial up creative passing concepts to get tight end (in-name-only) Brock Bowers loose against a 49ers defense that's 11th-best in yards allowed (1,472) and 17th most touchdowns given up (10).

"It's a talented bunch. Been together for a long time. Experienced bunch," Raiders offensive coordinator Andrew Janocko said of the 49ers defense. "It's a team that have a lot of familiarity with playing against them last year. They're well coached, well prepared. They attack the football. They're very opportunistic. So, it'll be a heck of a challenge for us against one of the best teams in the league."

With how disciplined 49ers defenders play, Las Vegas must win their blocking assignments to keep veteran quarterback Kirk Cousins upright to throw. And to ensure a more productive run game by establishing its own outside zone runs, the offensive line must anchor and push forward and not get stonewalled or driven backwards. A ground game that gains positive yards consistently allows play action to be truly effective — which is a staple of Kubiak's offense.

Sunday's clash marks the 16th time the Raiders and 49ers have met. The Niners lead the all-times series 8-7 (all regular season games) and the Las Vegas variant of the Silver & Black have played the Red & Gold only once: Back on New Year's Day in 2023 which the 49ers won 37-34. San Francisco is riding a two-game win streak with the 2023 meeting the latest one and a 34-3 victory over the then-Oakland Raiders in 2018 being the other. The Raiders last win over the Niners arrived in 2014 — a 24-13 win in Oakland.

"Another top five defense in the league. Feels like I'm saying that every week the last several weeks. I don't know who the bottom five defenses are, but we're not playing them. So, maybe someday. But what a challenge."

—Las Vegas Raiders quarterback Kirk Cousins on the Week 9 matchup

#2954. Binance breach puts custody at center of crypto infrastructure trade

The $6.3 billion Binance hack could accelerate a shift in how investors value cryptocurrency companies, putting a greater premium on custody providers and the infrastructure needed to securely hold and protect digital assets.

Much of crypto's evolution this year has involved companies moving past their reliance on trading volumes and transaction fees, to focus instead on building the infrastructure connecting digital assets with traditional finance. Last Thursday's breach adds a new dimension, bringing cybersecurity, custody, compliance and balance-sheet resilience into sharper focus.

Importantly, the Binance breach was not an indictment of crypto itself, or a case of leadership-driven fraud, as with FTX in late 2022. Instead, Binance's leadership moved quickly to contain the fallout, protect customers and commit the exchange's own resources to cover losses.

On Tuesday, Binance CEO Richard Teng told CNBC's "Squawk on the Street" that the company has replenished 98% of $6.34 billion in affected assets, and that it is still in the process of purchasing outstanding, more illiquid assets.

Management's response, alongside efforts elsewhere in the industry to boost confidence, helped prevent a security breach from escalating into a broader crisis of confidence. For example, Tether, Circle, Ripple and others moved quickly to freeze or restrict access to about $3 billion in stolen assets; Coinbase blocked transfers to compromised wallets and offered Binance direct assistance.

The hack could even help shape the next phase of the crypto infrastructure trade. The new winners may not just be the businesses with the most users, volume or assets under custody, but the ones that can also demonstrate they're able to protect those assets under extreme duress.

"Which company has the resources to invest in cybersecurity and protect customer funds? For a smaller company, it could be much harder because once these crypto companies become infrastructure plays, providing the well for tokenization … they need to have the compliance and control in place so that a similar hacking will not happen or can be minimized," Owen Lau, senior analyst at ClearStreet, told CNBC. "Larger companies have more resources that could give them some advantage."

While institutional investors have increasingly embraced digital assets, broader adoption depends on confidence that those assets can be securely held, accessed and transferred. The Binance breach highlights the importance of that infrastructure, and could increase demand for specialized custodians and platforms with established asset-protection capabilities.

Coinbase has operated a dedicated institutional custody business since 2018. Circle entered the custody business this year, after the U.S. Office of the Comptroller of the Currency granted it approval to operate as a trust bank. In the past few years, Ripple and Kraken have also launched custody businesses.

Still, smaller players compete in the space as well. Small cap crypto exchange stock Gemini Space Station has offered custody services since 2019. BitGo Holdings, founded in 2013, is one of the earliest players in institutional crypto custody and infrastructure, with a reputation for security and serving major institutions.

The shift could also drive consolidation as companies look to buy capabilities, deepen their infrastructure offerings and achieve the scale needed to compete, Lau said.

The more consequential structural shift may be the separation of custody from execution, according to Morgan Stanley.

"Crypto exchanges have historically combined trading, custody, and settlement within vertically integrated platforms, concentrating operational risk. Repeated security breaches could encourage investors to move assets toward independent custody while continuing to trade on CEXs," the firm's analyst Felix Stratmann said in a note Monday," referring to centralized cryptocurrency exchanges.

"This would preserve the role of centralized exchanges as liquidity and execution venues, while potentially benefiting regulated custodians, including banks, as institutional investors place greater emphasis on asset segregation, operational resilience, and investor protection," Stratmann said.

#2941. Democratic sweep puts crypto on defensive in Washington

Democrats' sweep of Congress likely resets the political calculus for crypto, ushering in a more adversarial approach in Washington and pushing industry-friendly legislation like the Clarity Act to the back burner.

With crypto critics like Sherrod Brown, D-Ohio, returning to the Senate and Sen. Elizabeth Warren, D-Mass., retaining significant influence over financial policy, analysts say the new Congress is likely to focus on scrutinizing President Donald Trump's personal crypto dealings over supportive industry legislation.

For years, crypto fought an uphill battle in Washington, asking for clear operating rules, but often landing on the receiving end of regulation by enforcement. That shifted after Trump's 2024 election victory, giving the industry a reprieve and seat at the policy table.

"We see Sherrod Brown winning in Ohio as damaging the crypto industry's political clout as the industry worked to defeat him," Jaret Seiberg, TD Cowen's Washington analyst, said in a note Wednesday. "We did not think Clarity could advance over the next two years. This makes us more confident in our prediction."

The crypto industry spent more than $200 million to sway the midterms, hoping to get leaders elected that could push a comprehensive crypto market structure bill over the finish line after the Clarity Act stalled in the Senate.

Seiberg emphasized that Democrats "will investigate Trump's crypto entanglements including World Liberty Financial" and said "the resulting political environment may make it impossible to advance a version of Clarity that the crypto sector could back."

Mike Townsend, Charles Schwab's managing director of legislative and regulatory affairs, said he expects "Democrats to push next year for a version of the bill that is much tougher on cryptocurrency companies."

The stakes extend beyond market structure legislation via Clarity. Stablecoin innovation; decentralized finance, or DeFi, development; banking access; tokenization; and sensible tax policy are priorities the industry sees as important to ensuring the U.S. remains competitive in the global crypto economy.

The industry will need continued bipartisan engagement to ensure these priorities aren't sidelined by changing political dynamics. And Democrats have historically been less supportive of crypto than Republicans, though that divide has narrowed as more Democrats embrace the industry.

The implications of Democrats' big win aren't all bad. Crypto has reached a level of financial integration that could be difficult to reverse, with its future increasingly centered on establishing crypto as financial infrastructure. Even with the Clarity Act potentially stalled until 2028, key financial regulators can help advance the industry through rule-making.

Coinbase's vice president of U.S. policy, Kara Calvert, points out that the election results point to a historic shift in the makeup of the incoming Congress, with a record number of pro-crypto voices expected to join its ranks.

"With last night's results, the 120th Congress is on track to have more new pro-crypto members than we've ever seen in any freshman class," she said. "Crypto is 100% bipartisan, and that's why the future of finance does not rest on either political party or a single election night. Democrats and Republicans alike know that clear rules protect consumers and keep innovation in the U.S."

As of Thursday morning, 19 candidates characterized as pro crypto by the advocacy nonprofit Stand With Crypto have been elected to the Senate versus eight Senate candidates it characterizes as anti crypto. In the House, 228 pro-crypto candidates have been elected versus 106 the group deems anti crypto.

Several races have not been called, but Fairshake — the super PAC launched in 2023 to support pro-crypto candidates regardless of party — has so far won 29 of the 34 races it spent on, per Reuters, including three of the six House candidates it prioritized by spending a baseline of $1 million on their campaigns.

Dante Disparte, chief strategy officer and head of global policy and operations at Circle, emphasized Circle's bipartisan approach and readiness for new Senate and House leadership, but said it "may take longer to get Clarity tabled again."

That bipartisan support may offer a blueprint for what comes next, but even if crypto legislation stalls, progress can continue, said Joseph Chalom, CEO of ether treasury company Sharplink.

"We expect the Clarity Act to remain on hold through 2028. Even so, there is a clear path forward for stablecoins … and for the tokenization of public equities under the SEC's five-year innovation exemption," he said. "Technological progress is rarely rolled back. Once the benefits of faster settlement, lower costs and markets that never close become clear to investors and institutions, this shift will be very difficult to reverse, regardless of who wins the White House in 2028."

Near-term, investors should expect more volatility as the market tries to figure out how crypto regulation may shift under a Democratic Congress.

The impact of the blue wave "should vary considerably across assets," said James Butterfill, head of research at CoinShares. "We would expect U.S. crypto equities to be more sensitive than bitcoin, particularly businesses reliant on new regulatory permissions. Established, compliant operators could even benefit from greater scrutiny of competitors. So miners who already have data centers set up are likely to remain in high demand by AI hyperscalers."

— CNBC's Talia Kaplan contributed reporting.

#2937. CNBC's experts on whether the AI trade is ending and what investors should do next

Amid a powerful rout in tech stocks, investors told CNBC on Thursday that something may be fundamentally breaking in the AI trade following the big success of Democrats in Tuesday's midterm elections and the threat they try to slow the data center buildout.

Democrats are more aggressively anti-data center than Republicans, and Wall Street is quickly redoing its math what any restrictions they implement could mean for the related companies' future growth.

"Right now, investors are questioning that $1.2 trillion of capex that we forecast for next year," Peter Boockvar, chief investment officer at One Point BFG Wealth Partners, told CNBC. "Well, maybe that's not going to happen."

Boockvar believes this could be the start of the end of the AI trade as we know it.

Whether it's completely over or not, investors need to be reassessing how they're planning to play the AI trade going forward, the analysts said.

The Nasdaq Composite dropped 5% on Thursday and fell into correction territory, off by more than 11% from its record high set last month. The iShares Semiconductor ETF dropped 11% on Thursday, its biggest decline since during the Covid breakout in March 2020. The Global X Artificial Intelligence & Technology ETF (AIQ) is down 6% on the day and down 15% over the past month.

"There has been a huge change in the outlook for AI infrastructure buildout," Eric Johnston, head of equities at Cantor Fitzgerald, told CNBC. "The entire economy and the consumer and earnings growth are all levered to that buildout."

The selling Thursday spilled over to non-AI stocks with the Dow Jones Industrial average down more than 1,600 points.

Josh Brown pointed to the 4.8% yield for the 10-year Treasury yield, saying a compelling risk-free rate is causing investors to reconsider taking the risk in these volatile AI stocks.

"You don't need to be at 80% U.S. stocks and have 60% of that exposure be tech," Brown said.

Many investors believe the AI trade isn't over completely, investors just need to get more selective.

"The AI buildout just got harder," said Rob Sechan, Newedge Capital Group Founder & Managing Partner:

"Nobody voted to make compute less scarce," Sechan said, citing Microsoft's Azure cloud business growing 48% year over year in its most recent third quarter. That level of growth suggests its capacity constrained, not demand constrained, he said.

"When new capacity gets harder to build, existing capacity becomes more valuable," Sechan said.

Richard Clode, portfolio manager at Janus Henderson, noted a few bright spots in tech on Thursday despite the highly correlated downward movements in the big tech indices.

"Motorola Solutions had some good results," he said. "We're seeing a little bit of rotation into some of the … internet names...Sony reported some good results, and again they were up strongly overnight."

"So there are some brief pockets of respite from this, but obviously pretty painful across the board today," he said.

Some experts believe the selling was overdone and investors could start nibbling.

This "indiscriminate selling" is a "buying opportunity," said Larry Adam, Raymond James chief investment officer. If investors "take a longer term perspective, which is what we do, I think the valuations at today's levels will tend to prove to be a good buying opportunity," he said, urging investors to look at the fundamentals.

Victoria Greene, chief investment officer of G Squared Private Wealth, said she saw some strong supports around the S&P 500's 200-day moving average and was on the lookout for a market bounce following Thursday's rout.

"Typically, the average return is plus about 180 basis points the next day," she said. "The question we have to ask ourselves: Is this a pullback in an uptrend, which is what I believe? Or is this the end-all, be-all [and] the wheels are coming off and everybody just needs to buy T-bills? I don't think we're there yet."

#2935. The Orlando Blueprint: What the Suns take into Houston and San Antonio

The Suns finally exhaled. One win, and a lot left to prove.

Thursday's 117-104 win over Orlando snapped a four-game losing streak, and after weeks of struggle, this fan base needed it just as much as the team did.

Now the hard part. Phoenix is 2-5, and the win came against a Magic team missing Paolo Banchero and Jalen Suggs and coming off two straight games under 100 points. Credit Phoenix for handling it on the second night of a back-to-back, but nobody is booking a playoff run off that.

This is the middle of a four-games-in-six-nights homestand that could make or break the season before it started. Portland, Houston, and San Antonio are three of the top six teams in the West. Orlando was supposed to be the "easy" one. Phoenix is 1-1 through the stretch, and the bar was 2-2. That means stealing one from the Rockets on Saturday or the Spurs right after. Easier said than done.

So forget the box score for a minute. The real value of the Orlando game is that Phoenix showed, for 48 minutes, what a winning version of this roster looks like. The question for this week is how much of it carries over.

Defense first, and it showed up in the turnover column

Orlando gave it away 25 times. Phoenix gave it away 14. That 11-turnover swing was the single biggest reason the Suns won. They pressured the ball, jumped passing lanes and turned stops into transition looks. Booker had 3 steals and Dillon Brooks had 2. When the defense gets set and forces mistakes, the offense gets easy shots and the whole night snowballs in the right direction.

Phoenix went 17-of-34 from deep. Orlando went 13-of-38. That is 12 extra points from distance on four fewer attempts. Those made up a 15-rebound deficit and 11 fewer free throws.

Devin Booker set the tone with 12 in the first quarter and 23 by halftime, finishing with 30 on 11-of-22 shooting, 8 assists, and a +16. Luke Kennard rescued the bench in the second quarter with 12 points on 3 threes. When Phoenix opened the third with sloppy turnovers, Brooks took the baton and finished with 24 points, 4 threes and a +12. Miles Bridges did the dirty work with 18 points, 13 rebounds and 5 assists.

What travels: the effort and the defensive intent. Ball pressure works against anybody. The Suns looked more engaged on that end than at any point during the losing streak, and that does not depend on who is across from them.

What probably doesn't: 50% from three. Phoenix is not shooting that every night, and a healthy, disciplined opponent will not hand over 25 turnovers. Strip those two numbers back toward normal and the Orlando game gets a lot closer.

What got exposed: the Suns were outrebounded by 15 and took 11 fewer free throws. Orlando went just 13-of-24 at the line and wasted the gap. Better teams will cash it in from the charity stripe.

It will take a complete, 48-minute effort to win these games. They had positive flashes that lasted 12-15 minutes in the previous three games leading up to the Orlando game, but were unable to put it all together and pull out wins.

The recipe is simple. Being active defensively, communicating relentlessly, and staying connected are the keys to winning games you aren't supposed to.

They won't beat Houston or San Antonio if they get killed on the glass or in the interior again. You get that leeway against Orlando. But the concept of winning a complete game like Orlando is a building block they can take into these next two games.

They may have to do it in a different way, but staying locked in for 48 minutes is the only way they stand a chance.

#2930. Jefferson, Whiteford carry torch for Monroe County Region football

Jefferson and Whiteford will carry the torch for Monroe County Region football.

Those were the only two teams to make it through district championship games on Friday, Nov. 6.

Jefferson ended Ida's season in Division 5 and Whiteford emerged from a tremendous Division 7 battle with St. Mary Catholic Central.

Meanwhile, Bedford and Gibraltar Carlson both bowed out in Division 2.

More: Bedford, Flat Rock, SMCC move on in state volleyball tournament

Here is a roundup of all the area high school football district championship game action.

NEWPORT – Hunter Barton caught touchdown passes of 53 and 32 yards and returned an interception 80 yards for a score to lead Jefferson.

"Ida is very good," Jefferson coach Rob Beaudrie said. "I told our team that the game would be won in the trenches and our guys played well. Our skill guys are going to do their thing, but this game came down to the offensive and defensive lines."

Drake Jones ran for 169 yards, a touchdown and a pair of 2-point conversions.

Luke Beaudrie ran 37 yards for a TD and threw a 21-yard scoring pass to Brayden Birkmier. Ryan Roberts tossed a pair of touchdown passes after Beaudrie left the game in the first quarter after tweaking his injured knee.

Justin Crozier ran for two touchdowns and Grant Pfeiffer one for Ida, which finishes 8-3.

Jefferson (10-1) will host Summit Academy for a regional championship on Friday, Nov. 13.

MONROE – Whiteford quarterback Tre Eitniear scored on a quarterback sneak with a minute left in the game and Andy Trejo added a 2-point conversion to settle a tremendous back-and-forth battle.

"I've been coaching football for a long time and I'm not sure I've ever seen both teams play as well on either side," Whiteford coach Todd Thieken said.

Brek Ruddy of Whiteford ran for 134 yards and two touchdowns. Eitniear finished with 107 yards on the ground and connected on 12-of-18 passes for 185 yards.

His 35-yard toss to Maddox Diesing set up the winning score and he hit George Thompson (34 yards) and Elijah Vance (5 yards) for TDs.

Sophomore quarterback Brady Reinhardt had a big night for SMCC, running for 269 yards and 3 touchdowns on just 9 attempts and throwing for 72 yards and a TD.

GROSSE POINTE – Bedford was shut out for the first time since Oct. 24 of last season.

"It was rough," Bedford coach Dave Mifsud said. "We moved the ball a lot in the first half, but we really shot ourselves in the foot with uncharacteristic mistakes. It really could have been a closer game at halftime and that would have taken the pressure off the defense."

The Mules trailed 14-0 after one quarter and 27-0 at the half.

Bedford, which finishes at 8-3, got great performances on defense from Everett Wingate (11 tackles, 2 sacks), Jaxson Lestock (7 tackles) and Alex Maples (7 tackles).

Keagan Dixon led the offense with 68 rushing yards on 8 carries.

GIBRALTAR – Two interceptions and a fumble were costly for Gibraltar Carlson in a close game.

"It was a hard-fought game tonight against an evenly matched team," Carlson coach Jason Gendron said. "Ultimately, we made too many mistakes and couldn't finish, which ended our tournament run."

Carlson's only points came on an 8-yard pass from Austyn Lawrence to Adrian Blackmon in the fourth quarter.

"I'm proud of how our players and coaches fought," Gendron said. "It was another good season for the Marauders. A special thank you goes out to our seniors for everything they contributed to the program over their four years."

#2929. Chiefs Market Movers: Who’s Trending at Midseason?

Week 9 marks the halfway point of the 2026 NFL regular season. The Kansas City Chiefs will take on the New York Jets, but before we get to that game, let's look at who's trending up (the Bulls) and down (the Bears) for the Chiefs through eight weeks:

Running back Emmett Johnson: The absolute star of the loss to the Denver Broncos, Emmett introduced himself to the NFL as a complementary back and as a kick returner. He's elusive, explosive, and high energy. He can catch the football and break big plays. From here forward, he should be sharing touches with Ken Walker (maybe put them both on the field at the same time)— and could be that dude at kick returns if they let him.

Wide receiver Rashee Rice: Rashee needs some good news this week. I'm sure that making this list will make up for what he's got going on. Anyway, I do think the arrow is pointing back up for the current #1 wide receiver on the roster. The Broncos defended him pretty well (in both games), but this is still a 1,000-yard-type guy if he's ever on the field for a full season again. Patrick Mahomes needs a reliable target who can get YAC—Rice is still perfect for that role, and he's likely as hungry as he's ever been. I wouldn't be shocked if Rice has over 100 yds this week and/or next.

Edge rusher R Mason Thomas: I am going to keep buying R Mason Thomas stock, because you just get the feeling it's about to take off. The rookie leads the team in pressure rate and is second in sacks (yes, with one…). His snap share has declined the last two games— and the pass rush struggled in both (losses). RMT might be a guy who gets QB sacks in bunches, and the team needs him. Watch to see if he's back to a bigger role this weekend.

Others trending in the right direction: Mansoor Delane, George Karlaftis, Kahlil Benson, Cyrus Allen, Nohl Williams, Felix Anudike-Uzomah, Ashton Gillotte, Andrew Armstrong, Chris Roland-Wallace

Wide receiver Xavier Worthy: I still believe in this guy, but my hope for a breakout season is dwindling. He has elite ability, but execution and awareness have been… suboptimal. The fact that the Chiefs are (allegedly) aggressive in the wide receiver market isn't a good sign for how they think of their recent 1st-round pick. At what point do they give up and make him a jet-sweep/tunnel-screen gadget guy only?

Kicker Harrison Butker: It's hard to pinpoint why a historically great (and clutch) kicker is suddenly costing his team every week. But it's easy to see that it's happening. Butker has missed more kicks than any other active kicker—and he's been paid more than most. His contract makes it tough to cut him, but bringing in other kickers (however controversial) might lead to a change at some point.

Offensive line coach Andy Heck: Along with head coach Andy Reid, the other Andy bears some responsibility for the struggles up front. Not only have they struggled to build enough depth to get through a season, but they also have fielded a group that gets penalized far more than is reasonable. On top of that, there are these mysterious issues that have led to a benching and a winding path to the Reserve/Injured list.

Not to mention the best center in the NFL having a bad snap or two nearly every game. Oh, and apparently, protection was also to blame for the short-yardage failures and even the missed field goal (according to Andy). The big uglies continue to be a problem that seems beyond the Chiefs' ability to solve— even after it has cost them dearly in multiple Super Bowls.

Others trending in the wrong direction: Jaylon Moore, Jack Cochrane, Harrison Butker, Nikko Remigio, Creed Humphrey, Kingsley Suamataia, Chris Jones

Jacob De Jesus: Chiefs Twitter thought it was funny that he was signed in the midst of wide receiver trade talks. He's not the answer at wide receiver, but he could be an elite punt returner if given a chance…

Ben VanSumeren: Given the short-yardage struggles, it's time to get behind a fullback.

Justin Fields: Given the goal-line struggles, it's past time to get Fields behind Trey Smith and Creed as needed for a push.

#2925. The AI ‘credit card’ is getting costlier as CoreWeave bond yield tops 16%

Debt markets are sending a message to AI data center builders: we're hiking the interest rate on your credit card and may put some new curbs on your credit limit.

That analogy comes courtesy of Jefferies global head of equity strategy, Christopher Wood, who put it in a note on Friday: "The most plausible way the AI capex arms race will end is that the credit card will be taken away."

That isn't happening yet, but credit markets have been spooked by the new political landscape — seeing more hurdles to data centers under Democratic governors in key states, along with a Democratic-controlled Congress.

The poster child for debt stress is neocloud Coreweave. The yield on its 2032 bond with a 9.625% coupon has climbed above 16%. Coreweave's five-year credit default swaps spiked above 1,289 basis points on Thursday, widening above their October peak, according to Deutsche Bank. The CDS eased 8% Friday to around 1,155.

"We've had plenty of warnings from the credit space," said Jim Reid, Deutsche Bank's global head of macro and thematic research, in a note on Friday. Debt and equities are now moving more in sync, he added, "so we'll have to see if credit goes any further."

Investors are demanding more compensation to insure against default among even companies with the strongest balance sheets and credit ratings, including Nvidia, Broadcom, and Amazon.com.

CDS on Nvidia debt is up 76% in the last month. Broadcom's CDS is up 49%, while Amazon's has gained 44%. Their CDS remain low on an absolute basis; Nvidia's spread stood at roughly 139 basis points, compared with about 298 bps for Oracle and roughly 1,100 for CoreWeave. A large increase from a low starting point does not imply the same credit risk.

Still, rising CDS are a sign that markets want more protection against default among even the largest and healthiest debt issuers. And rising CDS imply higher financing costs down the line.

The market is now grappling with the prospect that at least some data center spending will be delayed or scrapped under incoming Democratic governors in Ohio, Texas, and Georgia, the three states with much of the planned capacity.

Moratoriums could impact 45% of planned capacity in the U.S. with a large amount of that in Texas, according to Goldman Sachs. Texas governor-elect Gina Hinojosa has said she's not opposed to data centers but wants a moratorium until the state legislature passes protections for communities.

None of these means AI debt financing is collapsing. Goldman tallies five AI bond deals totaling $67 billion since October 29, pushing total hyperscaler debt issuance this year to $296 billion. Goldman forecasts $440 billion of hyperscaler debt issuance next year, plus an additional $300 billion of project-level issuance across investment-grade and high-yield.

The Trump administration is vowing to support data centers. National Economic Council Director Kevin Hassett told CNBC on Friday that he expects data center growth to continue as forecast, despite Democrats' big election wins.

The big question now is how quickly AI spending will generate enough cash for hyperscalers to keep investors comfortable with future capex plans. For the neoclouds, any sign of consolidation or retrenchment in capex could result in fewer or shorter contracts for data center compute or delays in prepayments.

Goldman analysts said hyperscalers collectively reported negative free cash flow in the third quarter as capital spending exceeded operating cash flow.

"Delayed monetization and growth in purchase commitments—which we view as additional and sizable uses of cash, separate from Capex—are the key risks to more debt issuance, subject to market conditions," according to the bank.

Peak AI debt issuance may now be closer at hand. Bank of America analysts estimates that AI-related credit issuance will rise 31% from 2026 to 2027, hitting $685 billion, including public and private debt. They then see it falling 23% to $531 billion in 2028.

As for the risk? "Downside to these forecasts exists from a potential slowdown in AI infrastructure projects on the back of the Blue Wave, which will reduce capex needs and thus debt market funding," BofA said in a note.

-CNBC's Nick Wells and Michael Bloom contributed reporting

#2924. Week 9 NFL picks against the spread, predictions for every game: Bears have momentum going into Sunday night vs. Buccaneers

Chicago Bears hype is about to get very loud.

This NFL season seems wide open. There isn't one great team yet and there are few trustworthy contenders who have emerged. The Bears seemingly saw it the same way when they traded two first-round picks for defensive tackle Jeffery Simmons.

And through one game, it seems like a transformative trade. The Bears had a fantastic performance on a national stage, shutting down the Seahawks in a 34-17 win. Chicago's Super Bowl odds took a leap, as did Caleb Williams' MVP odds. In a season dying for a dominant team to emerge, maybe it's Chicago.

The Bears' offense isn't a question. It has scored 30 points in every game Williams has started and finished (it did score just three points against the Vikings and Williams played three-and-a-half quarters, but that says something about Minnesota's defense). If Simmons has as big of an impact on the defense as it appeared on Monday night, Chicago will be looking really good. Nobody paying attention to the NFL hadn't seen how dominant Simmons is, but it was still impressive to see how quickly he fit in and made plays.

Chicago gets another close-up in prime time this week against the Tampa Bay Buccaneers on Sunday night. Surprisingly, the Buccaneers are far better analytically than their 3-5 record would suggest. They're fifth in DVOA, eight spots ahead of the Bears, and eight in net EPA (expected points added) per play. The Bears are strong in net EPA, coming in third.

The Buccaneers will be completely overshadowed in this game. The Bears will be the focus on Sunday night as they've emerged as one of the NFL's most dangerous teams. That's a reason they're a healthy 6.5-point favorite. That's a lot of points, and the Buccaneers might be secretly pretty good. That's why Buccaneers +6.5 is the pick. And if the Bears blow out Tampa Bay in a big prime-time window, they'll take over as the talk of the NFL, if they're not already.

The Bears are 4-0 in games that Caleb Williams started and finished this season.

Here are the rest of the picks against the spread for Week 9 of the NFL season:

The Bengals and Falcons go to Madrid, Spain, for this one. The Falcons are nearly impossible to figure out. They followed up a win over the undefeated 49ers by losing to a Buccaneers team that didn't complete a pass. That sums up Atlanta's season. We should trust Joe Burrow, who is on a heater, though there are more questions about Cincinnati's defense after it gave up 36 points to the Titans at home last week.

Maybe Joe Milton III gives Philadelphia a boost and the Eagles get their first win since Week 2. But since Jalen Hurts went on IR, the Eagles have lost all three games by double digits. It's not a good team in any way, and Milton is far from a sure thing. The Giants are off a bye and should be feeling good coming into this one.

The Broncos are the better team. A few things work against them in this spot: It's an early start on the East Coast, and the Panthers have three days rest advantage. The Broncos are also coming off an emotional win over the Chiefs and there could be a letdown. Denver has been much better at home this season. Carolina has faded after a good start but this is a decent opportunity for a win.

Give Miami credit for playing hard. Maybe at some point that effort starts to fade, but the last time they truly got blown out was Week 2. They lost by 14 points to the Chiefs, but Kansas City led by only seven points with three minutes left. It's possible the Lions score enough to cover, but remember that they're usually a different team outdoors.

Kansas City will enjoy the breather after facing the Seattle and Denver defenses on the road the past two weeks. This seems like the type of game in which the Chiefs could take out their frustrations on an opponent that does have some talent but has won only once since Week 1.

The Browns are likely going with Shedeur Sanders at quarterback because Deshaun Watson was put in concussion protocol. It's not like Watson was playing so well he's irreplaceable, but Sanders has thrown only three passes all season. The Saints are well rested coming off a bye. The Saints are not a great team this season, but this might be a spot for them to get right.

Is it time to trust the Rams? They have covered two games in a row without a sweat. Those wins came against mediocre (Raiders) or bad (Chargers) teams, but they did handle business. And the Commanders are mediocre at best and maybe just bad. Let's see if the Rams are truly back.

It is incredibly hard to trust the Cowboys, especially as a road favorite. But what we saw from the Colts last week in a 42-7 loss was troubling. There was no fight left in that team, which has lost key players to trades and knows its season is done. This could be a bad mistake, trusting a defense that can't stop anyone to cover almost a touchdown on the road. Taking the Colts at this point is even harder.

It's a lot for Houston to be favored by this much on the road, however, home-field advantage has been mostly eliminated this NFL season and the Chargers have no home-field advantage — even if that was still a thing. Houston is coming off a bye and still has hopes to revive its season. The Texans still have a 34.4% chance to make the playoffs according to DVOA. The Chargers are 0.0% on that list. They'll cover a spread at some point but it's hard to anticipate when it happens.

The Raiders have four losses, and they came against four of the better teams in the NFL: Chiefs, Patriots, Bills and Rams. The bad news is this week they get the Super Bowl favorites. The 49ers should be getting many players back after their bye. But the Raiders can move the ball and they're getting a lot of points.

With all the quarterback chaos this week, we get two teams that had a different quarterback in Week 8. Brissett was benched and traded, and Gardner Minshew II takes over. Sam Darnold has a quad injury and he'll be out, with Drew Lock taking over. Lock is a reasonable replacement, and Minshew isn't facing the Cowboys defense this week.

The Patriots have a lot of injuries and the Packers have a few days of extra rest. In a game that seems evenly matched, the small edges add up. Green Bay started the season very slow but has looked much better over the last four games, winning three and barely losing at Detroit. New England has also emerged after an early-season slump to win four of five. This is a really fun matchup, and even enough to feel OK taking the points.

This is an interesting game because the teams have opposite strengths. The Bills have a great offense and bad defense, and the Vikings have a bad offense and great defense. The Minnesota defense held the Bears to three points after all; Chicago has scored 30 points in every other Caleb Williams start. It will be a great test against Josh Allen, who is having another fantastic season. It's never enjoyable to be going against Allen, but getting 3.5 points with a defense this good seems right … as long as the offense does at least a little bit.

The Raiders scored on their first five possessions and led 35-0 before Fossil got on the board late in the first half.

Heralded junior quarterback Luke Rubley threw five touchdowns, two to Colt Jones. That sparked a Regis offense which had faced tough sledding against top teams like Grandview and Valor Christians to close the regular season.

Regis moves on for a rematch against No. 5 Grandview in the 5A second round Nov. 13. The Raiders are now 4-0 all-time against the SaberCats, with all four wins coming since 2022.

Fossil Ridge couldn't get anything going until it was too late against a Denver-area power.

Fossil quarterback Colton Jantz capped off another strong campaign, throwing TD passes to Caleb Camping and fellow junior Gavin Zanotelli . Zanotelli caught another touchdown from running back Will Hough off a trick play to open the second half.

Even after Fossil forced an ensuing fumble, the momentum was short-lived. Regis Jesuit's Andrew Mihm intercepted Jantz and returned it for a pick-six score.

It was a disappointing exit for Fossil after showcasing major improvement in October.

Fossil Ridge won four of its last five regular season games following a 1-4 start, beating rival Rocky Mountain, upsetting Legacy and pushing unbeaten Fort Collins to the wire before defeating Erie and Rock Canyon to return to .500.

This was still a bounce back season for the SaberCats, who went 2-8 last year while missing the 5A playoffs for the first time since 2020.

With Jantz and top target Zanotelli headlining the likely returners next season, Fossil could make some more noise in 2027.

The reigning 3A runner-up is out of the playoffs after No. 15 Windsor couldn't hold a fourth-quarter in the 3A football first round Nov. 7.

No. 18 Silver Creek scored two touchdowns in the final six minutes, including the go-ahead touchdown pass with nine seconds left to leave with a 17-13 win.

The Wizards held a 13-3 lead in a defensive battle heading to the fourth quarter behind Robert Trotter Jr. TD tosses to Bekum Helm and Devonte Fontana.

But the Raptors clawed back, first with a rushing score to trim the lead.

After a stop, they drove down the field (with a fourth-down conversion) and sat inside the Windsor 10-yard line in the final moments. Braeden Macchia rolled right and found a wide-open Wyatt Mills for the winner.

Silver Creek (5-6 overall) will face No. 2 Holy Family in the 3A second round next week.

Windsor (5-6) had surged down the stretch, winning four of the last five regular season games after a 1-4 start and finishing 5-1 in league play.

But the Wizards couldn't keep it rolling, failing to win a playoff game in a season for the first time since 2021.

Chris Abshire covers high school and community sports for the Coloradoan.

#2916. Brandon Aiyuk: I’m back to 100 percent and looking to get reinstated

Brandon Aiyuk will not play for the 49ers again, but his future is in question.

The wide receiver arrived at the team's business offices in a van with his agent Friday afternoon to meet with the 49ers and presumably negotiate a resolution.

"Obviously, Brandon is here to meet with the team," Drew Rosenhaus, Aiyuk's agent, said before the meeting, via video from David Lombardi of the San Francisco Standard. "Fortunately, he's feeling good physically. He's healthy. He's ready to get back to work. Don't really have any comment about where he's going to play. We'll keep that between us and the Niners. But obviously we're here with all the right intentions."

Aiyuk last played in a game on Oct. 20, 2024, when he tore the ACL and MCL in his right knee. The relationship between the team and the player fell apart after that, and the 49ers voided the guaranteed money due to his unwillingness to rehab at their facility.

The 49ers placed him on the reserve/left squad list on Dec. 13, 2025. Rosenhaus filed a reinstatement request on Oct. 27, but the NFL still hasn't reinstated him.

"Nice to see you all," Aiyuk said. "I'm feeling great, feeling healthy. It was a long recovery process, a long rehab process. But I'm back to 100 percent and looking to get reinstated into the National Football League."

49ers coach Kyle Shanahan said earlier in the day he "[couldn't] care less" how the meeting went since "it's been over for our football team for a long time."

#2915. Steelers’ Christian Kuntz named NFLPA community MVP

This article originally appeared on SteelersNOW.com.

Pittsburgh Steelers long snapper Christian Kuntz has won the NFLPA Community MVP Award for his community work in his hometown of Pittsburgh.

Kuntz led two community events in one week. The Steelers' long snapper donated $10,000 to Pittsburgh Morrow school to help open a new resource room, which will provide free shoes, uniforms, winter jackets, hygiene products, and food for more than 200 students and 70 economically disadvantaged families each month.

That same week, his Kuntz 4 Ki6s Foundation hosted its first Halloween Bash, welcoming 80 local kids for trick-or-treating, pumpkin decorating, food, and a chance to pick out a free costume.

With Kuntz being named Community MVP, the NFLPA is donating $10,000 to the Kuntz 4 Ki6s Foundation.

The Kuntz 4 Ki6s Foundation was started in 2023 by Kuntz. His foundation is dedicated to promoting youth leadership and engagement, fostering mentoring relationships, and supporting underserved communities.

#2913. Lakers’ new big man accepts JJ Redick’s challenge, takes team to new heights

Luka Dončić is dominating headlines with arguably the greatest start in NBA history. But it's no secret that Walker Kessler's blue-collar work in the paint is just as essential to the Lakers' success this season.

Before the Lakers played the Clippers on Oct. 28, coach JJ Redick issued a challenge to Kessler after he fell short of grabbing double-digit rebounds in his first four games of the season.

Los Angeles Lakers center Walker Kessler (14) and guard Luka Doncic defend Miami Heat forward Bobby Portis during a game Monday, Nov. 2, 2026, in Los Angeles. AP Photo/Jayne Kamin-Oncea

"Well, I think where he can continue to grow with us, you know, [with] per 36 numbers, per-minute numbers, he's not rebounding at the rate [we expect], and obviously that's a area of concern for us right now," Redick said. "So need to be him doing better, particularly on the defensive glass.

"… I remember my rookie year. B.J. Armstrong was an agent at Wasserman at the time and came down to Orlando to check on me. I remember when we were at the game, he was talking about Dwight [Howard]. He said, 'You know what Dwight's superpower is?' I said, 'No, I don't.' And he said, 'He can rebound outside his space. He can rebound outside his airspace.' And I've seen Walker do it. That's where I think he can give us the biggest boost right now defensively. We want him to protect the rim. We want him on the interior."

Redick wants Kessler to be everywhere. He's the heart of the Lakers' defense. He's wants one of the league's greatest rebounders and rim protectors to leap to the next level.

Following an 11-point, nine-rebound performance against the Clippers, he went on to have three straight double-doubles, averaging 13.6 points and 14.3 rebounds in a loss to the Warriors on Oct. 30 and wins over the Raptors on Nov. 1 and Heat on Nov. 2.

"I think his effort to go get the ball has been good," Redick said after the Lakers' 138-117 victory over the Heat on Monday. "I think he's boxed out really well."

Kessler is averaging 14.4 points, 10.2 rebounds and 2.7 blocked shots for the Lakers (7-2), who are in second place in the Western Conference.

Kessler is seventh in the NBA in rebounds. He's third in blocked shots.

They rank 21st in defensive rating (112.9) but first in offensive rating (121.1). Much of that is due to the team's steep drop-off when Kessler is off the court.

When Kessler is on the court? The Lakers' defensive rating is 110.1. When he's sidelined? It's 117.2, per Cleaning the Glass.

Kessler's impact on the Lakers has been notable.

His screening creates open shots for Dončić, who's averaging a league-leading 38.5 points, 8.1 rebounds and eight assists, and Austin Reaves, who's averaging 23.3 points on career highs in field-goal percentage (53.1) and 3-point percentage (42.9).

Los Angeles Lakers center Walker Kessler dunks against the Sacramento Kings in an NBA basketball game in Sacramento, Calif., Wednesday, Nov. 4, 2026. AP Photo/Scott Marshall

Kessler's presence in the paint has emboldened the team's perimeter defenders to be aggressive and take risks, which has led to the Lakers ranking No. 1 in opponent turnovers (166).

Kessler is ranked No. 1 among defenders who have taken at least 50 shots within 6 feet this season, holding opponents to 45.1% shooting.

But it's no secret that Kessler is still figuring things out with the Lakers.

The Lakers acquired Kessler in July in a sign-and-trade with Utah for two first-round picks and two first-round pick swaps on top of a four-year, $130 million deal.

Miami Heat center Bam Adebayo handles the ball as Los Angeles Lakers center Walker Kessler (14) defends on Monday, Nov. 2, 2026, in Los Angeles. AP Photo/Jayne Kamin-Oncea

He's still working his way back from missing all but five games last season because of a torn posterior labrum in his left shoulder that required surgery.

Kessler is one of nine new players on the Lakers, and it's going to take time for him to learn Dončić and Reaves, in addition to developing into the defensive beast the team needs.

The Lakers need Kessler to be at his best to be a championship-level team.

He has steadily improved this season, though he stumbled a bit in Wednesday's 116-103 win over the Kings, finishing with 15 points, eight rebounds and a game-high four blocked shots. Kessler acknowledged that Domantas Sabonis (16 points, 17 rebounds) got the better of him, saying, "[He] definitely got my tail kicked a little bit in the first three quarters."

For Kessler, this is a learning process. There's going to be bumps in the road. Redick has preached patience.

And it's clear Redick wants him to reach new heights.
